A full last leaf starts a new one for a key at its end, so keys put in in order fill every node. `first`, `last`, `pop_first`, `pop_last` and the nearest key on either side as Java's `floorKey`, `ceilingKey`, `lowerKey` and `higherKey` are methods; `subMap`, `headMap` and `tailMap` are a `for` loop over `m.range(a..b)`, with `a..=b`, `a..`, `..b` and `..=b`, only in a loop, so there's no view to keep; a range that ends before it starts panics, as `subMap` throws. Checked against Java 25 over 1.4 million random operations, and 1.3 to 7 times as fast as Rust's `BTreeMap` at each operation measured. | Phase 3 |
| D113 | 2026-10-28 | PriorityQueue: Java's binary heap, element for element | `PriorityQueue<T>` is Java's `PriorityQueue`, which vanilla drains its scheduled block and fluid ticks from, and the chunks with ticks due. Java's iterator goes along the heap's array, and vanilla's `removeIf` on a chunk's ticks removes with it, so the array shows; every change moves the elements as Java's does, up past only parents an element comes before, down to the right child only when it comes before the left, and a taken-out place filled by the last element, down or else up, so after the same changes each element is in Java's place, and a loop and printing go in Java's order. It's a list type marked as a heap, so it shares a list's struct and glue, in a template the emitter includes once for each element type. `push`, `pop`, `peek`, `remove(x)`, `contains` and `clear` are methods; the iterator's `remove` is `remove_marked(marks)`, a loop marking elements and then one call that replays Java's iterator, elements that went up past it kept, as Java's `forgetMeNot` keeps them, and taken out after the end; there's no comparator, and an element type with that `Order` stands for one. `pop` moves the gap down Java's path to the bottom and the last element back up, which ends where Java's sift does with about half the compares; elements that compare as one number, including structs of up to 128 bits of integers, whose members the emitter packs side by side into one 64- or 128-bit compare, go down two levels a step, the four grandchildren loaded with the children. Checked against Java 25 at 710 checkpoints over 3.4 million random operations, and 1.2 to 2.5 times as fast as Rust's `BinaryHeap` at each operation measured. | Phase 3 |
| D114 | 2026-10-28 | Arenas: second-class lists, no lifetimes | `Arena` gives memory in chunks and frees it all at once with `reset()`, and `a.list<T>()` makes an `ArenaList<T>`, which is a `List` in all but where its memory comes from. Calx has no lifetimes, so an arena list is second-class: it can only be a local variable or a parameter that isn't `own`, never a field, a return value, an element or a generic argument. It's made only as the whole value of a `let`, from an arena in a variable or a field, holds only `Copy` elements, can't be assigned or copied, and keeps its arena (by field path, as a `for` loop keeps what it goes over) from changing or moving until its block ends. A `parallel for` takes memory only from its own arena, as in `arenas[k]`. The arena's state is on the heap so it can move with what holds it, and growth is inline, in place when the list is the newest block. A tick of new lists each time runs 2.1x faster than new `List`s and as fast as lists kept and cleared by hand, and 6.9x faster than new Rust `Vec`s. Generic methods take explicit types too: `p.with<f64>(2.5)` | Phase 3 |
| D115 | 2026-10-28 | Actors are structs; std.mail; Send; @track_caller | Supersedes the `actor struct` and `send dest msg` syntax in the spec. An actor is a plain struct that a `parallel for` runs, holding `std.mail`'s `Outbox<D, M>` and `Inbox<M>` as fields. At the barrier, `Mail<D, M>` collects outboxes in ascending sender id and gives letters back in order of sender, then of sending (D20). It panics if a sender comes out of order or twice in a round, so the order is checked rather than promised. Why: D49 made `parallel for` over owned values the only parallelism, the capture check already keeps each outbox to its iteration, and an implicit current-actor outbox would be hidden state. `actor` and `send` are ordinary names now. `Send` is a derived bound: every type has it except one that is or holds a pointer or an arena list, and a generic parameter has it only with the bound. `@track_caller` is the one attribute the compiler knows: the function's panics name its caller, which it gets as a hidden last C parameter, and one tracked function passes its caller on to another. Any other attribute is an error wherever it's written. Before, one on a method or a field was silently ignored | Phase 3 |
| D116 | 2026-10-28 | `for x in mut xs`: an exclusive view of each element; parallel loops over elements | `for x in mut xs` goes over a list, an array or a deque, and `x` is a `mut` view of each element, a pointer in C. Nothing in the body reaches `xs` except through `x`: reading it is an error as well as changing it, which is stricter than a loop over a field (D106) and the same as Rust's `iter_mut` borrow. So the list's length and memory are read once, and a body that never changes `x` is an error, as an unchanged `mut` parameter is. `parallel for` also goes over a list's or an array's elements, with or without `mut`, each iteration getting its own element, so the capture check needs no index for it. Sets, ranges and text can't be changed in place; map values with `in mut` wait. | Phase 3 |
| D117 | 2026-10-28 | Text slicing and Java's root-locale case mapping | `s[a..b]` and every range form take byte offsets, panic unless in order, in the text and on rune starts, and give a `str` for a `str` and a `String` for a `String`, copied only where it's kept; `to_lower_root` and `to_upper_root` are Java 25's `toLowerCase` and `toUpperCase` with `Locale.ROOT`, final sigma by Java's word rules included, from tables generated by asking Java about every code point; `find` and `find_last` give byte offsets | Phase 3 |
| D118 | 2026-10-28 | Native libraries, again (supersedes D32) | No vendored libraries: Calx's runtime has its own MD5, SHA-1, SHA-256 and HMAC-SHA256 (std.hash), random bytes from the system, AES-128 with AES-NI and CFB8, and RSA with PKCS#1 v1.5 padding (std.crypto), all written from the RFCs and FIPS documents and checked against Java 25 black-box; zlib and lz4 are written the same way from RFC 1950, RFC 1951 and the LZ4 block format; TLS, which only the HTTPS calls to Mojang's session server need, comes from the system: WinHTTP on Windows and libcurl loaded at run time on Linux. CFB8 decrypting runs 16 blocks at once, which a library's generic CFB doesn't | Phase 3 |
| D119 | 2026-10-28 | Decompressors follow vanilla's library, not the format | Where vanilla's decompressor accepts bytes its format calls invalid, Calx's accepts them the same way, so a world or packet vanilla reads, Calcite reads: std.lz4 takes what lz4-java 1.10.1's LZ4BlockInputStream takes, a match 0 back as zeros, a last match nearer the end than 12 bytes, and the last-literals rule measured on lz4-java's buffer, which grows with the blocks; std.zlib already gives Inflater's outcomes. Checked by fuzzing both ways, 500,000 broken LZ4 streams and 240,000 deflate ones, with no difference | Phase 3 |
| D120 | 2026-10-28 | Stack traces on every panic | After the message, a panic writes its Calx frames innermost first, Java's way (`at module.Type.fn(file:line)`), read from the line tables every build has, so they cost nothing until a panic: dbghelp on Windows, the program's own DWARF on Linux, inlined calls as frames of their own so debug, release and both systems agree, and the runtime's frames, the C library's and generated glue (nodebug) left out. A `#line` is repeated after each C line of a statement, so every call is at its Calx line. Inside a `parallel for` the trace is the one running the loop on one thread would write, whichever threads ran what (D90): the chunk that panicked writes its frames down to its body, each inner loop's owner the frames from its loop to its own chunk's body, and the thread that started the outermost loop the frames outside it, each part with the output it belongs to | Phase 3 |
| D121 | 2026-10-28 | Threads and typed queues for services | `std.thread` gives Calcite's core services their threads (D49). `Thread.spawn(task)` needs `unsafe`, as the clock does (D88), since what a thread does when depends on timing; it moves `task`, whose type implements `std.thread.Task` (`fn run(own self)`) and is `Send`, to a new thread that calls `run`. `join()` waits for it and uses it up; a dropped `Thread` carries on and ends with the program, and memory it still holds isn't reported as leaked. `Receiver<T>()` makes a queue of `T: Send` and its first receiving end; `sender()` makes a sending end, and `share()` another end of either kind. `send` moves a message in, `next` takes one or gives `none`, and `wait` waits for one and gives `none` once the queue is empty and every sender is gone; all three change the queue, so they need a `var`, `mut` or `own` end. The queue is a ring that grows, so `send` never blocks; messages left when the last receiver goes are dropped then, and ones sent after it when they're sent, outside the queue's lock, since a message can hold an end of its own queue. The ends and threads are built-in types that can't be copied, compared, shown or hashed. The runtime counts a queue's ends, which is how it frees it, but no Calx value is shared by them, so D76 holds: each message has one owner the whole way. A queue used, or a thread joined, in a `parallel for` body is an error, and reached from a function the body calls it panics with the caller's place, with one thread as with many (D21). A `parallel for` on a spawned thread runs on it alone, and `set_workers` there panics. Once a thread exists, printing takes a lock, so lines never mix, and the first thread to panic or exit ends the program while the others wait. The runtime is calx_thread.c, linked only into programs that use it | Phase 3 |
| D122 | 2026-10-28 | Plain functions as values | A function's name without a call is a value of type `fn(A, mut B, own C) -> R`, written as the function is declared without the names: the modes are part of the type, a function that can fail has `-> R ! E` and gives a result from each call, and an `unsafe fn` or a C function has an `unsafe fn(...)` type that only `unsafe` code calls, the only kind whose parameters or result can be pointers. `Type.f` is a value too, with what a method is called on first; a generic function's types come from `f<...>` or the type expected. A call through a value follows the direct call's rules (modes, D48) without names or defaults; `s.f(x)` is always a method, so a field's function is called as `(s.f)(x)`. A function value captures nothing, so it's `Copy` and `Send`. It can't be compared, shown or hashed, since two functions with the same code can be one, and a `@track_caller` function can't be a value. In C it's a function pointer whose parameters are passed as a direct call's, so it points at the function itself. Closures, which capture, come later with their own types | Phase 3 |
| D123 | 2026-10-28 | Libraries a program opens while it runs, for plugins | `calx build --lib` builds a `.dll` or `.so` from a main file without `main`, exporting the functions marked `@export`, which can't be generic, `@track_caller`, in an impl, or named `cx_`. A program opens one with `unsafe { Library.open(path) }`, since its code was never checked, and `lib.find<fn(...)>(name)` gives the function as a value only when its type matches, compared by a hash of its shape, which is the type with the fields and variants of every struct and enum in it, so two builds of a shared module that differ are refused rather than misread. A library has no runtime: under `-DCX_LIBRARY` each runtime function the header declares with `CX_FN` is a pointer the program fills in from its own when it opens it, in the header's order, so allocations, output, panics, threads and the leak check are one, a library's calls into the runtime are one indirect call, and the program's own stay direct. Its one exported symbol, `cx_library`, carries a hash of the compiler and the header, and a library from another build is refused. Dropping a `Library` leaves it loaded and `unsafe { lib.close() }` unloads it, since a function found in it would call nothing. Stack traces read each library's line tables, its PDB, which `calx build` now puts beside what it builds, or its own DWARF. A result passes through an `unsafe` block that holds only its call (D91) | Phase 3 |
| D124 | 2026-10-28 | Attributes run as native code, with cached answers | A library's attribute is a `pub fn` marked `@attribute`, whose first parameter is the declaration (`StructDecl`, `EnumDecl` or `FnDecl` from std.meta, read mode), whose others are literals (`String`, an integer type, `bool`, or an enum without data), and which returns `Generated` or `Generated ! String`, the reason being the error at the use. Not an interpreter, as the spec first said: the compiler builds the attribute modules and what they import into a debug program of their own, in process, and runs it once for all of a program's uses, with the declarations on standard input and each answer marked on standard output, so what an attribute prints goes to standard error instead. Native code makes attributes as fast as any Calx, gives panics their traces, and needs no second implementation of the language to keep in step. The answers are cached under a hash of the compiler, every use and the attribute modules' text, so an unchanged program doesn't run them (48 ms check against 101 ms after an edit to an attributed struct); the cache keeps 256 programs' answers. The generated code is text, parsed into the use's module and checked like the rest, with errors at their line in the generated file and a note at the attribute (editors get them at the attribute), and its items may go unused. Arguments are checked against the parameters' exact types before anything runs. An attribute reads attributes on fields only if it lists their names in `@attribute(...)`, and gets their arguments as written. An attribute can't be used in its own module or one its code needs. `calx expand` prints the code without checking. Determinism is expected but not enforced until build inputs exist | Phase 4 |
| D125 | 2026-10-28 | Raw strings | `#"..."#` has no escapes, keeps braces and backslashes, and interpolates with `#{expr}`; `##"..."##` holds `"#` and interpolates with `##{}`, and any number of `#`s works. A `"#` followed by `{` doesn't close one, so `#"f("#{x}")"#` reads as generated code wants. One whose `#"` ends its line is a block: its text is the lines up to a line holding only the closing quote, whose indentation every line loses, character for character; a line with text indented less is an error, the newline before the closing line isn't text, and CRLF becomes LF. Otherwise a raw string stays on one line. Chosen over Rust's `r#""#`, which has no interpolation, and Swift's `#""#`, which this follows but whose `\#()` interpolation reads worse in templates of Calx code, since attributes generate code as text | Phase 4 |
| D126 | 2026-10-28 | Packets declared once, with IDs from the table | Each packet is a struct named as vanilla's class without `Packet`, such as `ServerboundHello`, marked `@packet(Bound.Serverbound, "hello")` with vanilla's name for it. The attribute, from `protocol.codegen`, writes its `Wire` reader and writer from its fields in order (`@varint`, `@max(n)` and `@rest` say how a field is written) and its `Packet.id(state)`, its place in each state's list in `protocol.ids`, or -1, since a packet such as `keep_alive` is in several states with a different ID in each. An enum marked `@packets(State.Login, Bound.Serverbound)` reads a frame: its variants are named after their packets, which gives each one's ID, and its `read` fails as vanilla's `PacketDecoder` does on an unknown ID, a packet that fails to decode, and bytes left over, with vanilla's messages. A wrong name is a compile error that suggests the right one. `protocol.ids` is Calx that `oracle/datagen/calx_tables.py` writes from the tables and is committed, so Calcite builds without the jar, with a test that fails when it's out of date; it holds only names and IDs, which every compatible server encodes. Reading follows vanilla's limits and order of checks; writing a value over a limit makes the writer's `problem` say so, as vanilla's encoder throws | Phase 4 |
| D127 | 2026-10-28 | The server's first network layer | `server.network`'s `Network` owns the connections the server accepts on a `std.net` `Net`, in a list indexed by each `Conn`'s slot, and runs on the server thread. Each poll's events go to `handle`, which reads frames with `protocol.frame` and answers the handshake, the status and ping, and the old pings of clients before 1.7, as vanilla's listeners do; `sweep` closes connections that sent nothing for 30 seconds, on a clock the caller passes in, so tests set the time. A connection the server closes keeps its slot, marked closing, until its `Closed` event, so later events for it are dropped and it's never closed twice. A broken frame or packet in these states closes the connection with no answer and no log, since vanilla's encoder has no disconnect packet in them and logs the reason only at debug level. The log is `server.log`: Mojang's console pattern in local time, with the thread vanilla names (`main`, then `Server thread`), and code below `main` returns `Entry`s for its caller to log, so it stays testable. The status's JSON, components' JSON and the icon's checks are tested against what vanilla's own codecs, Gson and `PngInfo` give. Calcite doesn't ask for Mojang's EULA, since it isn't Mojang's software. This single-threaded start is what `P4.6` replaces with the network layer of D33 | Phase 4 |
| D128 | 2026-10-28 | Logins, ticks and the server's language | The server thread runs 20 ticks a second: between ticks it polls the `Net` until the next one is due, and `Network.tick` moves each login on, as vanilla's listener's `tick` does. Login packets are handled when they arrive, as vanilla's are on its network thread, so the profile goes out on the next tick after the hello, as in vanilla. Each connection has a `protocol.codec` `Codec`: its frames, AES-CFB8 once encryption starts and compression once it's on, in vanilla's pipeline order, so the compression packet goes out uncompressed and everything after it compressed. Where vanilla's `Connection.exceptionCaught` ends a login, Calcite sends `disconnect.genericReason` with the Java exception vanilla would throw, as its `toString` writes it, worked out from the reader's message, which is vanilla's own; the listener's `disconnect` logs `Disconnecting ...`, and every login logs `... lost connection: ...` once it's gone, with the address as Java writes it. Text the server makes from components, such as those log lines, comes from `chat.language`, which has no translations until P5.8 reads en_us.json from the jar, since D30 keeps Mojang's text out of the repos; until then a key is its own text, as in vanilla for a key its language hasn't. | D129 | 2026-10-28 | Online mode and Mojang's services | Online mode authenticates as vanilla's does: a 1024-bit RSA key pair made at start, a 4-byte challenge, the secret's AES-CFB8, and the session server asked with the signed hex SHA-1 vanilla's `Crypt.digestData` gives. Vanilla asks on a thread per login; Calcite asks with std.http, whose requests run on threads of their own (D118), and polls each one on the server's tick, so the server never waits, and acts on the answer as the authenticator would, with its lines on `User Authenticator #N`, logged even once the player has gone. `auth.services` fetches the discovery document and the services' public keys on authlib's schedule, one at a time as its one discovery thread does, but the keys once, where vanilla fetches them twice for no gain. What an answer means follows authlib's `MinecraftClient` and `hasJoinedServer`, status by status and error by error. Answers are read as strict JSON, with Gson's reading of a number or boolean where a string goes; the few malformed answers that throw an exception authlib doesn't catch, leaving the login to time out, mean the player didn't join. The environment comes from `-Dminecraft.api.env` and `-Dminecraft.api.discovery.host` on the command line, as vanilla's system properties, and `Services.offline` is authlib's `createOffline`, which fetches nothing. The rig's JSON parser became `json.tree`, so Calcite has one JSON reader | Phase 4 |
| D130 | 2026-10-28 | calcite.properties and Velocity's modern forwarding | Calcite's own settings go in calcite.properties, beside server.properties, in the same Java Properties format and read by the same `load`, so server.properties holds vanilla's settings and nothing else, as a vanilla server's would. A server without one writes it, each setting at its default with a comment, and a setting that can't work stops the server before it listens. The first is Velocity's modern forwarding, the proxy support players behind Velocity expect, written from the protocol: after the hello the server queries `velocity:player_info` with the newest version it reads, 4, which carries no chat key since a 26.3 client sends its chat session in play; the answer is checked with HMAC-SHA256 in constant time, then read with the login's own `Reader` and limits, and older versions are read as far as the profile. Forwarding takes the place of online mode, since the proxy authenticated the player, and its profile and address are the player's from then on. Paper and Velocity are GPL, so neither is read: the messages and setting names are Calcite's own, and a player the server turns away is told why in plain words | Phase 4 |
| D131 | 2026-10-28 | The vanilla data pack, registries and tags | The server reads vanilla's built-in data pack from Mojang's jar, as D30 and D61 say: `pack.vanilla` downloads the pinned jar from Mojang the first time into `cache/`, writing it beside its path and then renaming it, and checks its size and SHA-1 against the pin each time it reads it, then checks the jar bundled inside against the SHA-256 the bundler's `versions.list` gives. The pin is compiled in from oracle/versions.json by calx_tables.py, as the built-in registries are from the data generator's report. `pack.zip` reads a zip from memory, as Java's `ZipFile` reads one; the jar and every data file in it, 8,369 of them, read in about 85 ms, the SHA-1 check taking 100 ms more. `pack.resources` lists files as vanilla's resource manager does, in `Identifier` order, path first, with later packs over earlier ones, so user data packs can go on top later. A synchronized registry's entries take IDs in the order of their files' identifiers, and each registry's tags are read, merged and built as `TagLoader` does: a later file adds to a tag unless it says `replace`, a file that's JSON but not a tag file leaves an empty tag, and tags build in the order of `DependencySorter`, with Java's `HashMap` and Guava's `HashMultimap` order through `JavaHashMap`, so the same tags fail, with the same lines, when a pack has a cycle or a missing reference. A `registries` rig probe checks the result against vanilla's own loading: all 129 cases match, the order of every synchronized registry and the 773 tags with their entries in order | Phase 4 |
| D132 | 2026-10-28 | NBT, components as NBT, and configuration | `nbt.tag` reads and writes NBT as vanilla's `NbtIo` does, in network form and file form: the thirteen tag types in one enum, a compound in a `JavaHashMap` so its keys are written in the order Java's `HashMap` holds them, a list of mixed types wrapped in compounds under an empty key as vanilla's `ListTag` writes it and unwrapped when read, and `NbtAccounter`'s sizes, quota and depth with its messages. Errors carry the exception's simple name and message, wrapped as vanilla wraps them: a broken read inside a tag is `ReportedNbtException: Loading NBT data`. Printing follows vanilla's `toString`, keys sorted by UTF-16 unit and quoted only when they must be. The `nbt` rig probe checks binary input against vanilla, all 32 cases; reading SNBT comes later. Components go to clients as NBT, as `ComponentSerialization` sends them since 1.20.3, with the same fields in the same order as their JSON. Configuration runs as vanilla's listener does: its tasks in order, the keep-alive every 15 seconds with the time as its challenge, and the code of conduct and resource pack tasks when the server has them. An exception in a handler vanilla runs on the server thread is logged and the connection goes on, and one in a handler it runs as the packet is read ends the connection, so a client that sends a packet at the wrong time gets what vanilla does. What is the same for every player (the brand, the features, the known packs question, the registries without their contents and every tag) is encoded once at start in `server.configuration`, where vanilla encodes the registries and tags again for each player, so configuring a player costs copying bytes through its codec. The brand is `calcite`, since a client shows it and Calcite isn't vanilla. A client without vanilla's `minecraft:core` pack would need the registries' contents, which need each registry's codec, so for now it is told so and disconnected. The spawn is ready on the first tick until there are worlds, and a client that finishes configuring is told in play's disconnect that play isn't written yet | Phase 4 |
| D133 | 2026-10-28 | Links, the resource pack and the code of conduct | server.properties gives configuration the bug report link, the resource pack and the code of conduct, read as vanilla's `DedicatedServerProperties` and `DedicatedServer` read them, each warning and error with vanilla's text, on `main` in vanilla's order: the pack's warnings as the settings load, the link's and the code of conduct's after the data packs. The link is checked by `java.uri`, a URI check written from black-box tests of JDK 25's `java.net.URI`, not from its source: the rules for each part were found from probes, then the check was compared with the JDK on 80,000 random strings until each gave the same scheme or the same exception, index and message, including the message's input, which Java shows with each run of spaces as one and none at the ends. The one simplification is inside an IPv6 address, where Java has several messages and Calcite says `Malformed IPv6 address`, since a server's link is almost never one and a mistake there is still refused. The code of conduct's files are listed sorted, as `std.fs.list` gives them, so the language a client without its own or `en_us` gets is the same on every system, and a file is refused if its real path's folder isn't the folder's real path, which `std.fs.real_path` gives; vanilla compares with the folder's absolute path and so refuses every file when the server's folder is reached through a link, which is a bug. | D134 | 2026-10-28 | Registry contents wait for typed registries | A client is sent a synchronized registry entry's contents only when it doesn't know the pack the entry came from. A 26.3 client always knows `minecraft:core` at `26.3`, and vanilla's feature packs, so while Calcite's data is vanilla's alone, no vanilla client needs them, and one that answers otherwise is refused with a message saying so. The contents are each entry decoded with its registry's codec and encoded with the network codec, which keeps only some fields, leaves out defaults, gives each number its type and writes components as NBT; turning the pack's JSON into NBT field by field would only get that right with a description of every type anyway. The server needs those types for itself: dimension types for play, biomes for worldgen, damage types for combat, enchantments for items. So each registry gets a typed reader as the work that uses it arrives, with its network writer beside it, checked against vanilla's `packRegistries` in the rig, and contents are sent once every synchronized registry has one, with the world's own data packs (`P5.8`), the first time an entry can come from a pack no client knows | Phase 4 |
| D135 | 2026-10-28 | Every Calx thread's stack is 8 MiB | A Calx program's main thread, each thread `Thread.spawn` starts, each `parallel for` worker and the HTTP client's threads all get an 8 MiB stack, on every system: Windows programs are linked with an 8 MiB main stack, and the runtime starts every thread with that size (a reservation on Windows, the pthread attribute elsewhere). Windows gives a thread 1 MiB unless told, and glibc's and macOS's threads take theirs from the system, as little as 512 KiB, so how deep a program could recurse depended on the system and the thread, and a debug build of the SNBT parser ran out on Windows before vanilla's depth limit of 512. A stack is address space, so a thread takes memory only as deep as it goes. With 8 MiB, the SNBT parser goes past 4,096 levels in a debug build and 6,144 in a release one, 8 times the limit, and tests/thread/stack.calx checks a recursion of about 1.5 MiB (release) and 5 MiB (debug) on the main thread, a spawned one and the workers | Phase 4 |
| D136 | 2026-10-28 | A stack overflow is a panic | A call that goes past the end of its thread's 8 MiB stack (D135) panics as any other bug does: the output so far, `panic at <stack>: the calls went deeper than the thread's 8 MiB stack holds`, the trace, and status 101, on every thread and inside `parallel for` as the iteration's panic. Before, the program died with no message (0xC00000FD on Windows, SIGSEGV on Linux), which for a plugin's runaway recursion would have meant no crash report. The panic runs on 256 KiB kept for it, 8 times what it was measured to need: on Windows a vectored handler with that much of the stack held back by `SetThreadStackGuarantee`, and on Linux a SIGSEGV handler on a stack of its own (`sigaltstack`), which panics only for a fault just below the thread's stack and leaves any other to the system. Linux builds are compiled with `-fstack-clash-protection`, so a frame bigger than a page touches each page in turn and can't step over the page below the stack into other memory, as Windows' `__chkstk` already makes sure. A trace writes a frame that repeats right after itself once, then `... repeated N more times`, so an overflow's trace is a few lines, not 128 | Phase 4 |
| D137 | 2026-10-28 | `Box<T>` is a list of one in memory | `Box<T>` owns one value on the heap, so a type can hold itself through it, as the spec had it. It's built as a list that always holds exactly its one value: the same pointer, length and capacity, freed, copied, compared, hashed and shown by the code lists already have, so it needed no new runtime and no new generated functions. `Box(v)` is checked as the list `[v]` of the box's type, and `b.value` as its element at index 0, which is read without a bounds check since it's always there, and is a place as a field is, so it's read, changed, passed as `mut` and matched where it is. What makes it a list stays hidden: it isn't indexed, looped over, written as a literal or given list methods, it has no `JavaHash`, and its value can't be moved out, only copied, since the box keeps it. A box is 24 bytes where a bare pointer would be 8, and its value costs one allocation as a list's would; boxes hold recursive data such as a component's hover text or a syntax tree, which isn't on a hot path, so the smaller layout can come later without changing the language. `Box` is a built-in name, as `List` is | Phase 4 |
| D138 | 2026-10-28 | Play's first world is the classic flat one, encoded once | Until worldgen and the world folder (Phase 5), a player joins the classic flat world: bedrock, two dirt and grass, from y −64 to −61, in plains, everywhere, generated nowhere and saved nowhere. Every chunk is then the same but for its coordinates, so world.flat writes the chunk's data, heightmaps and light once, as the server starts, and a chunk's packet is its ID, its x and z, and those bytes, so sending a chunk costs a copy. That's the shape chunk caching (P5.11) will have for real chunks. Block and biome IDs come from the registries as they load, not from constants, so the world stays right if the data changes. The light is what vanilla's engines give a flat chunk, written out, until lighting (P5.7). Chunks as near as each other go by x, then z, where vanilla's hash set gives an order of its own, so a session is the same each run | Phase 4 |
| D139 | 2026-10-28 | A program's build ID is a hash of its C and its runtime | `std.build.build_id()` gives 32 hex digits that a program keys its caches on, as Calcite's boot cache is keyed. The compiler hashes the program's generated C, the runtime's header and every runtime file it links, but not the build's flags, so the same code has the same ID in debug and release and on any machine, and any change to a module the program uses, std's included, or to the runtime makes a new one. A source hash alone would miss a compiler that writes different C, and the executable's own bytes differ between builds of the same code (paths, PDB stamps) and cost a read and a hash at start. The ID is a constant in a file of its own, written and compiled only for a program that calls `build_id`, so other programs don't change and a program that does pays one tiny compile when its code changes | Phase 4 |
| D140 | 2026-10-28 | The vanilla data from the client jar's stretch, and the boot cache | The server no longer downloads the 62 MB server jar. Mojang's client jar holds the same data/ and language files, byte for byte, but compresses each file on its own, where the server jar compresses its inner jar as one 23 MB stream, so the 5,570,520 bytes from the first of those files' local headers to the end of the last can be read alone. fetch.py pins the client jar and that stretch's offset, length and SHA-256 in oracle/versions.json, and the server takes it from `cache/` if it kept it, else from an installed game's copy of the jar, read with `std.fs.read_part`, else from Mojang with an HTTP range request, taking a whole jar if the server ignores the range; each is checked against the SHA-256. `Zip.walk` reads the stretch file by file, as Java's `ZipInputStream` does: its files keep their sizes in data descriptors, so each is inflated with `std.zlib.decompress_stream` to find its end, and kept, so reading it later costs a copy. From an installed game, a first start takes about 50 ms: 2 ms to read, 13 ms for the SHA-256, 24 ms to inflate 10.5 MB, and 7 ms for the language and registries; with the download, about 0.5 s. What the data makes, the language, the registries with their tags, the packs' known names and the lines loading them logged, then goes into `cache/boot.bin`, keyed by `std.build.build_id()` (D139) and the data's SHA-256, written beside its path and renamed. A later start reads that in 2.5 ms and logs the lines again, so the console looks the same. The RSA key pair, 6 to 12 ms of the start, is made on its own thread, which only an online login waits for. A start from the cache is listening 4 ms after `main` begins, under Pumpkin's 5 to 8 ms, and as soon after the process starts as a C program that does nothing but listen. User data packs and plugins will key the cache by each file's size and time, and load in parallel on a miss | Phase 4 |
| D141 | 2026-10-28 | Unsigned chat first, as vanilla sends it to a player without a chat session | Chat comes in two steps, as P4.5 plans. This one is everything a client without a chat session gets from vanilla, which is also what every player gets with `enforce-secure-profile=false`: `SignedMessageChain.Decoder.unsigned` makes each message `PlayerChatMessage.unsigned`, so the player chat packet carries no signature, the body is stamped with the server's clock, not the client's, with salt 0 and nothing seen, and the console tags it `[Not Secure]`. The network has no clock of its own, so the server's loop gives it the wall clock with `set_clock` before it hands over what arrived, as vanilla reads `Instant.now()`, and tests set it. The checks around that are the ones vanilla makes of every message, signed or not, in its order: the last-seen update first, then the characters, then chat visibility, then the spam count once the message has gone out. `LastSeenMessagesValidator` is ported whole, pending messages and all, though nothing adds one until signed chat, so its messages, its `BitSet` length and its checksum, Java's `Arrays.hashCode` folded with 31, are tested now. The chat types' decorations come from the data packs' chat_type files and go into the boot cache, since the console's line is the chat type's translation, and commands' messages will need the others. With no command tree yet, a command gets brigadier's unknown-command failure as vanilla's `Commands.finishParsing` builds it at cursor 0, which is what an empty tree gives every input | Phase 4 |
| D142 | 2026-10-28 | Signed chat is checked as vanilla checks it, with Calx's own RSA signatures | A chat session's key is checked with the services' keys, `SHA1withRSA`, and each message with the player's key, `SHA256withRSA`, in vanilla's order and with its messages: the chain's place and time stamps, the last-seen window, the signature cache that packs what a recipient's client has, and the limit of 4096 messages unseen. A signed command can't match a command, since Calcite has none with arguments to sign, so one with signatures is refused as vanilla refuses a mismatch. The RSA is std.crypto's, checked against JDK 25's keys and signatures, and checks a 2048-bit signature in 19 µs to Java's 22. A key is read only in DER's shortest form, of 512 to 4096 bits, which every key Mojang issues is, rather than in any DER Java takes, which no vanilla client sends. The status is rebuilt every 5 seconds, as vanilla's is, so its `enforcesSecureChat` comes on once the services' keys do | Phase 4 |
| D143 | 2026-10-28 | How the server sends | std.net's writes wait for the next poll or `flush`, so each connection's bytes since then go to the system in one send. The server flushes once a tick ends and after each event it handles between ticks, as vanilla suspends flushing for a tick and flushes after each packet between them. A flush of up to 1 KiB of packets is encoded on the tick thread; more goes to a codec thread, with the connection's `Encoder`, which moves there and back with each batch, so the threads share nothing and a connection's bytes keep their order, one batch away at a time. A `Waker` wakes the poll when a batch is back. Codec threads start as they're needed, up to one fewer than the processors. What arrives is still read on the tick thread, since a client's packets are few and small, until regions have their own threads. A tick's 64 flat chunks then cost the tick thread 2 µs, not 685 | Phase 4 |
| D144 | 2026-10-28 | The input log | D21's log, for the server so far. Everything from outside goes through `server.link`'s `Link`, in the order the server asks for it: the poll's events, each with the time the loop handled it, and the bytes the server reads; each tick and sweep with its time; the wall clock; a connection's address; the system's random bytes, such as a random seed's, the login's challenges and the session ID; the key pair; and Mojang's services' answers. Live, the link passes them through, at the cost of a branch. Recording, it writes each as a record, a tag byte and its body, after a header with the format's version and the game's, in protocol.wire's encoding; the server's loop takes what's new after each turn and a thread of its own appends it to a file, so the tick never waits for the disk, and a log cut off in a record is read up to it. Replaying, the loop's inputs come back through `Network.replay_step` and each answer where the server asks for it, so a server made the same way goes the same way with no network, threads or clock; one that asks for something else than the log has next stops there, saying what. The key pair is logged as its primes, restored in 1.5 ms, so what clients encrypted with its public key opens again. Woken events aren't logged, and a replay has no codec threads, so every flush is encoded inline, which sends the same bytes. To check a replay, the log also holds each packet the server sent, as its connection, ID, length and XXH32, and each line it logged; the replay compares what it sends and logs with those, as two sequences, since lines are taken when the caller chooses, and reports the first difference and how many. Hashing costs about 1 µs a 7 KB chunk while recording. The files read at start, the settings, the icon, the code of conduct and the vanilla data, aren't logged: a replay runs in the recording's folder, and one with other settings shows where they change what the server does. Every network test records its scenario and replays it at the end. A log holds what players sent and the server's private key, so it's off by default and for the admin alone | Phase 4 |
| D145 | 2026-10-29 | Vanilla's codecs read every packet the tests are sent | Elliot's first join with a real 26.3 client failed: `level_chunk_with_light` had 4123 bytes left over. Calcite wrote the light masks as arrays of longs, while 26.3's `ByteBufCodecs.BIT_SET` writes a `BitSet` as `toByteArray`'s bytes. Every test had passed, because the test clients read packets with Calcite's own codecs, which share every mistake the server makes. Now `test.play` and `test.configuration` write each packet their clients get to `build/packets/`, and the vanilla adapter's `PacketCheck` reads them with the 26.3 jar's own protocol codecs, bound to the vanilla data pack's registries. Each packet must decode, leave no bytes over, and encode back to the same bytes. Two kinds are compared as values instead, since vanilla doesn't fix their order: `set_time`, whose clocks are keyed by identity-hashed `Holder`s, and `update_tags`, whose registries are keyed by identity-hashed `ResourceKey`s and whose tags are `HashMap`s the decoder sizes differently from the server. `src/test/run.py` runs the check whenever the adapter is built, and CI builds it. This doesn't run a vanilla server: it only loads the jar's packet classes, as the rig's probes do. Comparing what a running vanilla server sends, packet for packet, is still CAL-42. Why: a test only proves anything about the protocol if the other end is vanilla | Phase 4 |
| D146 | 2026-10-29 | Login throttling is opt-in | P4.9 asks for connection throttling, which vanilla doesn't have. `login-throttle` in calcite.properties is the fewest milliseconds between two tries to log in or transfer from one IP, and a try sooner is refused at the handshake. Every try starts the wait again. It's 0, off, by default, for three reasons: vanilla lets every try in; behind a Velocity proxy every player comes from the proxy's address, so it's ignored with `velocity-forwarding`; and players reconnect quickly after a kick or a resource pack. Status pings aren't throttled, since they're cheap and the server list sends them often. The times come from the link, so a replay refuses the same tries. Why: a public server can turn it on in one line, and nothing changes for anyone who doesn't | Phase 4 |
| D147 | 2026-10-29 | logs/latest.log | Every line goes to the console and to logs/latest.log, in Mojang's pattern, as the log4j2.xml in `com.mojang:logging` has log4j write it. The file is archived to `logs/<day>-<N>.log.gz` at the start and at the first line of each day. The rules for when it rolls, its name and its number, 7 a day, were measured, not read: log4j 2.26.0 ran with Mojang's configuration in set-up folders with a fake clock, and `test/log_file.calx` checks each case against what it left. The file is written on a thread of its own, as the input log is (D144), and each archive is compressed on another, as log4j's asynchronous actions are. So the server thread never waits for the disk, and the boot stays at 5 ms. `main` closes the log however the server ends, so every line is in the file. Two differences: log4j's second, empty roll at the first line after the start, which can delete an archive, isn't copied (rig/differences/log-second-roll.md), and lines end in `\n` on Windows too (log-line-endings.md). A replay logs to the console alone, so it never rolls the log of the server it replays. To read a file's last-written day, Calx's std.fs gained `metadata` and `set_modified` | Phase 4 |
| D148 | 2026-10-29 | The console's own style | On a terminal the console has Calcite's own look and words: the time dimmed, a colored tag for the level or for what happened (`READY`, `JOIN`, `LEAVE`, `CHAT`), the thread only when it isn't the server's own, later lines aligned, and lines that tell the admin nothing new not shown. In a file or a pipe it prints vanilla's lines exactly, since hosting panels and wrappers read them for `Done (...)! For help` and `joined the game`. `console-style` in calcite.properties (`auto`, `calcite`, `vanilla`) overrides that, and logs/latest.log is always vanilla's. Each `Entry` carries a `Look` beside vanilla's message, so tests and replays still see vanilla's text, and the words are Calcite's own, never copies of Mojang's en_us text. A terminal is told by Calx's `std.io.is_terminal`, which on Windows also turns on the console's reading of ANSI codes, so codes never show raw. Why: the console is the first thing anyone running Calcite sees, and vanilla's is noisy, with four lines for offline mode and two for every join, while every tool that reads it still gets exactly what it expects | Phase 4 |
| D149 | 2026-10-29 | server.properties written back | As it starts, Calcite writes server.properties back as vanilla's `Settings.store` does, with every setting `DedicatedServerProperties` reads, each as it read it, and every other key as it was: byte for byte what the 26.3 jar's `--initSettings` writes, but for the line ends. That needs Java's `Properties.store` (keys sorted by `String.compareTo` since JDK 18, `saveConvert`'s escapes, raw UTF-8), `Date.toString` with the zone's tzdata abbreviation, which Calx's `std.time` now gives, and Gson's reading and writing of `generator-settings`. Two differences: `\n` line ends and, on Windows, the zone named by its offset, since Windows has no abbreviations (platform); and an `entity-broadcast-range-percentage` that isn't a number taking its default instead of stopping the server (bug). Malformed `generator-settings` still stops it, as in vanilla, since a world generated from settings that were dropped can't be undone. The secret is drawn from the system's generator, not the link, since it never reaches play; a replay writes nothing. Why: a new server should get a full file to edit, as vanilla's does, and tools that read or diff server.properties see vanilla's | Phase 4 |
| D150 | 2026-10-29 | The console's card | D148's look read as a generic Java logger: 16-color tags, whole messages painted, a loud time and no structure. Calcite's style now shows a card when the server is ready, in place of the startup lines: `calcite` as a badge in the calcite block's off-white with the logo's ink (Calcite's color is the block's, not the banner's amethyst), Minecraft's version and the time to ready, then rows for the address, how accounts are checked, the world and the players, and a note naming the settings files it made. After it, each line is a faint time, a mark for what happened (`→` join, `←` leave or turned away, `▲` warning, `×` error) and the words, with chat as a bold name; threads aren't named. Colors are exact 24-bit from GitHub's Primer palette, which reads on dark and light backgrounds, falling back to xterm's 256, the 16 basic ones on the Linux console, and none with `NO_COLOR` or `TERM=dumb`. A fresh folder's missing server.properties is vanilla's `ERROR` line in vanilla's style and in latest.log, and the card's note in Calcite's. Why: Elliot found D148's look not good enough, and the first thing anyone running Calcite sees should say what they need, where players join and whether accounts are checked, at a glance | Phase 4 |
| D151 | 2026-10-29 | The start's thread is `ServerMain` | Correcting D127 and D133: vanilla's lines before the server thread starts are on `ServerMain`, not `main`. Mojang's server jar is a bundler, whose own `Main` unpacks the libraries and starts the server's `Main` on a new thread of that name, so its latest.log reads `[ServerMain/WARN]: Failed to load eula.txt`. D127 named the thread from the decompiled server `Main` alone. Calcite now logs those lines on `ServerMain`. Why: the line's thread is part of vanilla's pattern, and tools that read the log, or diff it with vanilla's, see it | Phase 4 |
| D152 | 2026-10-29 | The console's commands, and stopping | Calcite read nothing from standard input, so a hosting panel's `stop` did nothing and a server could only be killed. Now a `Server console handler` thread reads its lines as vanilla's `BufferedReader` over an `InputStreamReader` in UTF-8 does, which `java.text`'s `decode_utf8` and `LineReader` match: compared with JDK 25 on 200,000 random inputs, each split into two pieces at every point, with none different. Each line goes through the link as a `COMMAND` record, and the console's request to stop, which CAL-55's input line makes at Ctrl+C as vanilla's shutdown hook does, as `HALT`, so a session that ends either way replays. The server runs them between ticks, as `tickConnection` runs console input. The command tree has only `stop`, so everything else gets brigadier's error as `Commands.finishParsing` sends it, with vanilla's text from the language data; the other commands are P6.6's. Stopping does what `stopServer` does that Calcite has: `Stopping server`, then each player is told `multiplayer.disconnect.server_shutdown`. Vanilla doesn't wait for the disconnects to go out, and its lost-connection lines depend on whether a connection closed before `handleDisconnection` ran; Calcite waits up to 3 seconds for each to close and logs each, so a player is told before the process ends. Calcite saves nothing yet, so vanilla's saving lines aren't logged, rather than claiming what it doesn't do. Ctrl+C without CAL-55's bar still ends the process as a signal does; handling it is for later | Phase 4 |
| D153 | 2026-10-29 | The console's dashboard (CAL-55) | With `console-dashboard=bottom` or `top`, a bar of TPS, MSPT, CPU, RAM, players, chunks and uptime stays pinned to the terminal, redrawn once a second, with the input line beside it. **Who draws.** One thread, the dashboard's, owns the terminal's output: the log's lines, the echo of what's typed and the bar all go through it, so nothing else writes between a frame's bytes and nothing tears. Each frame is one write, wrapped in synchronized output (`ESC[?2026h`) and with the cursor hidden while it draws. A reader thread hands it the raw bytes typed, and a beat thread wakes it on each whole second since the start. The server thread sends it each tick's time and the nanoseconds it was busy since the last, and once a second the players and chunks: two channel sends a tick, so the tick doesn't wait on the terminal. **Where it goes.** At the bottom there's no scroll region: the dashboard asks the terminal where the cursor is (`ESC[6n`), keeps the row the log's next line goes on, counting the rows each line wraps to, and at each frame clears the bar, writes the log's new lines there and draws the bar under them. So the log fills the screen down to the bar, then scrolls the screen as any output does, and the scrollback holds the log alone, where pinning the bar with a region or with inserted lines left blank rows in it. At the top a region does it (`DECSTBM`), since a bar that scrolled with the log would leave copies of itself; the content on the screen is moved down under the bar, not pushed off it, and the log's cursor is kept with `DECSC`. A terminal that doesn't answer where its cursor is in two seconds gets the bar at its foot. **A resize** can rewrap and scroll the terminal's lines, and Windows Terminal moves the cursor with them but not a cursor saved with `DECSC` (checked: a `DECRC` after narrowing the window lands mid-text). So at the bottom the bar is cleared from the cursor, which is on the input line, and drawn again from where the terminal then says it is, as far below the log's end as it was: the rows between are blank lines, which move but never wrap. At the top the screen is drawn again in place from row 1, without scrolling it, with as many of the log's last 256 lines as fit under the bar at the new width, so it's right whether or not the terminal rewrapped. Both were checked in Windows Terminal with players joining, by screenshots of the window. **What it counts.** TPS is `(n - 1)` ticks over the time from the first of the last 100 to the last, rounded to a tenth and at most 20; MSPT is the mean of their busy times, the tick and what was handled before the next. Both count from now instead once the last tick is more than 2 ticks old, so a stuck server shows at the next second, not when it gets going again. CPU is the change in the process's CPU time over the change in the clock and the processors (CLX-50). The thresholds, TPS under 19.5 or 15, MSPT over 40 or 50 ms, RAM from 80% or 90% and CPU from 80% or 95%, are where a server admin should look, and where it's falling behind. The chunks are those the players' views hold, counted once each, which costs the server thread a set of every player's view once a second; with real chunk loading it'll be the chunk map's count. **Where it's not drawn.** Only on a terminal it can drive: standard output a terminal, `TERM` not `dumb`, the size known, raw input on, and not under Pterodactyl or Pelican, whose console is a terminal the panel reads lines from, which they show with `P_SERVER_UUID`. Anywhere else the console is the log as before, so panels, files and pipes read vanilla's lines. **Ctrl+C.** In raw mode Ctrl+C is a key, not a signal, so it asks the server to stop, as vanilla's shutdown hook does, and the server ends with status 0 once its players are told. A second Ctrl+C, or one while it stops, ends the process at once with 130, as a shell gives for Ctrl+C, with a muted line saying so; `at_exit` puts the terminal back however it ends. **Not done.** Top mode loses the scrollback of what scrolls out of the region, as every terminal does with one; bottom mode is the default to suggest for it. A line wider than the terminal is counted as wrapping at its width, each code point one column, so wide characters, such as CJK, can put the log a row out until the next resize | Phase 4 |
| D154 | 2026-10-29 | The bot benchmark (P4.10, CAL-44) | D34's fixed bot scenario: 100 players join a fresh release server, 20 a second, in offline mode with vanilla's compression threshold of 256, view and simulation distance 10. Each loads every chunk of its view, then walks at walking speed, 0.21585 blocks a tick, on a heading of its own, its index times the golden angle, so they spread out and the server streams chunks the whole time, and says something every 10 seconds, which all 100 are sent. It runs 45 seconds. **The bots** are `src/rig/swarm.calx`, made from Calcite's own protocol code, on one thread. That's light enough beside the server not to crowd it, and they can't disagree with it on the format, which the network tests and vanilla's codecs (D145) check, not this. They answer as a client must to stay: the teleport, keep-alives, each chunk batch, and a tick end every 50 ms, without which a second move counts as two in one tick and ends the connection. **What's measured.** The server's `--tick-times FILE` writes, as it ends, each tick's start and the server thread's work since the tick before: the tick and what it handled between. That's the number the console's bar shows as MSPT, and it costs a clock read a turn of the loop only when asked for. The steady part is from 2 seconds after the last bot had its chunks to the end, and gives MSPT's mean, p99 and max and the TPS; the joins give each bot's time from connecting to its last spawn chunk, and the MSPT while they joined. The system gives the process's CPU time and peak memory, from `GetProcessTimes` and the peak working set on Windows and `wait4`'s usage on Linux. **The result** is the median of 5 runs, each a fresh process, after one start that isn't measured, so a new build has made its boot cache. `bench/run.py --record` writes it to `bench/results/bots-<platform>.json` with the commit, the compiler and the machine, and a later run compares each number with it and names what's more than 5% worse, which D34 says to look into before more work lands. The scenario is fixed so results compare across commits; other bot counts and lengths run for a look and aren't recorded. Vanilla isn't run beside it yet: that's CAL-42's comparison, waiting on the EULA question, and Phase 8's benchmarks | Phase 4 |
| D155 | 2026-10-29 | Views up to 1024 chunks, and `force-view-distance` (CAL-57) | A player's view stopped at 127 chunks, 52,653 of them, whatever `view-distance` said: a client asks for its view distance in one signed byte, as vanilla's `ClientInformation` reads it with `readByte`, and the view is that, from 2 to `view-distance`, as `getPlayerViewDistance` makes it. Nothing on the server stopped there: no array, queue or packet. **`force-view-distance`** in calcite.properties, `0` and off by default, gives every player that view, from 2 to 1024 chunks, whatever the client asks for, and the login packet says it, so the client keeps chunks that far out. Each player is logged with what they asked for and what they're sent. It's for testing the server, as vanilla has nothing like it, and with it off a view is vanilla's. Vanilla also keeps `view-distance` from 2 to 32 (`ChunkMap.setServerViewDistance`), which Calcite doesn't yet (CAL-58). **A tick's chunk work grows with the chunks that change, not the view's area.** Before, the sender sorted every chunk waiting each tick and dropped one by searching them, a move walked every chunk of both views' squares, and the console's count put every chunk of every view in a set: at 1024 chunks, 3.3 million each. Now a sender holds a bit for each chunk the client has, in a square that wraps around as the client's chunk cache does, so a chunk of the view without one is waiting, and a whole view comes in with nothing to mark. The order chunks go in, nearest first, by distance squared, then `x`, then `z`, as before, is one list of offsets packed in longs that every player shares, made a ring of about 65,000 at a time as a sender reaches its end, and sorted by their bytes. A sender keeps where it's up to: the key before which nothing is waiting. A move takes it back by the distance moved, as nothing waiting can be nearer by more, and a chunk that comes in nearer takes it back to that chunk. A view is walked a column at a time, each column's ends from an integer square root, for a move, a chunk that leaves going before one that comes, as the two can share a bit, and for the console's count, which sorts every view's columns packed in longs and merges those of one `x`: 22 µs for one player at 1024 chunks, 3.2 ms for 100. `test/chunks.calx` checks the sender against the old one, which sorts everything, through 600 random moves, jumps and view changes, and a view of 300 chunks across its rings. With the bots of D154 asking for 64 chunks a tick, the most a client may, one at 1024 chunks walking, one at 10 times that, and ten walking, no tick took more than 1.5 ms; the first join takes 1.1 ms, where building the whole order and queueing the view at once took 57. Over 10 minutes at 1024 chunks, one bot walking was sent 767,817 chunks, a quarter of its view, 1,280 a second, in ticks of 0.086 ms on average and 1.55 ms at most, with the server's peak memory at 38 MiB. A client fills a view of 1024 chunks in about 43 minutes at 64 chunks a tick, which is the protocol's pace. At view distance 10, D154's benchmark, over 10 runs on Windows, went from 0.508 to 0.463 ms a tick on average, 1.787 to 1.521 ms at p99, and 2.98 to 2.54 s of CPU | Phase 4 |
| D156 | 2026-10-29 | Lines of standard input, and bytes as Java reads them, in Calx's std (CLX-52) | Calx could read standard input only as bytes, so a program that reads lines had to split and decode them itself, as Calcite's console did in `java.text` (D152), and the guide's first program (CLX-36) would have had to as well. **`std.io.Lines`**: `next()` gives the next line or `none`, split as Java's `BufferedReader.readLine` splits them, at `
`, `
` or `
`, a line that ends at `
` given at once, as `readLine` gives it, and a `
` after it skipped even when it comes in the next read. It reads through the same runtime function as `std.term.read`, so a Windows console's input comes as UTF-8 from its UTF-16, not as bytes in the console's code page. Rust's `lines()` ends lines only at `
` and fails at bytes that aren't UTF-8; Java's rules are the ones vanilla's console reads by, and a line always reads. **`std.text.utf8.decode_lossy`**: bytes as text with U+FFFD in place of what isn't UTF-8, as `new String(bytes, UTF_8)` and an `InputStreamReader` give it. Which bytes one U+FFFD stands for is `sun.nio.cs.UTF_8`'s, measured with JDK 25 before it was written: mostly Unicode's longest piece that could start a character, but a surrogate, `ED A0 80` to `ED BF BF`, is one for all three bytes, as Java decodes it before rejecting it. Valid text is checked by `String.from_utf8` on a copy first, so it costs the copy and one check. `tools/unicode/utf8_check.py` compares both with JDK 25: 200,000 random byte strings, and 40 random streams fed to `Lines` in random pieces, with none different, on Windows and Linux. Calcite's `java.text.decode_utf8` and `LineReader` gave the same results, and gave way to these (CAL-59): the console thread reads with `Lines`, and the dashboard's input, the session server's answers, as authlib's `IOUtils.toString(stream, UTF_8)` reads them, and the protocol's strings decode with `decode_lossy`. A string a client sends that isn't UTF-8 was refused; vanilla's `Utf8String.read` decodes it with Netty's `toString(UTF_8)`, which is `new String(bytes, UTF_8)`, and counts its units after, so now Calcite does too | Phase 4 |
| D157 | 2026-10-29 | Sorting integers, faster (CLX-25) | Supersedes D96's sort of integers and its numbers. **Short runs.** A quicksort's run, or a spread's bucket, of 32 integers or fewer is sorted by Batcher's odd-even merge sort for 4, 8, 16 or 32 elements, the smallest that holds it (5, 19, 63 and 191 comparators), with no branch on a comparison, where D96 insertion sorted runs of up to 16 and every element cost a mispredicted branch. The network sorts the run's first element and those after it, or the list's last elements if that would pass its end, so it takes in some neighbors, which it can't move out of place: those before the run are no greater than anything in it, those after no less, and integers that are equal are the same value. A whole list shorter than 32 is sorted by the network of its length, or of the next one up on a copy filled out with the type's greatest value. `tools/sort_networks.py` writes the networks and checks them on lists of zeros and ones, every one up to 16 elements and 200,000 at random of 32. **The spread.** Keys of 4 or 8 bytes in lists of 192 or more, when 5 or more bytes differ or the list has at most 4,096 elements for each byte of key, are put into buckets by the top bits that differ, about a bucket per element, or one per 4,096 in a list of more than 65,536 so each bucket is sorted in cache. A bucket of more than 32 is sorted by itself, one of 5 to 32 by a network, and one insertion pass copies the list back and sorts the rest, as no element goes past the start of its bucket. Otherwise a pass per byte that differs, from the lowest, when the list has at least 128 times the square of their number, as D96 did, and a quicksort below that. Values that would mostly fill one bucket are quicksorted instead: a sample of 32, taken at multiples of the golden ratio so a list that repeats can't fall in step with it, refuses when more than 16 share a bucket, and the counts when more than half do. **Lists in reverse.** An unstable sort of a list whose first two elements are in reverse checks and reverses it in one pass, from both ends at once, 16 elements a side, each block checked before it's swapped, so every element is read and written once, where D96 read it twice; if the order breaks, the elements swapped so far are just a different order for the quicksort to take. A stable sort keeps D96's pass and reverse, as a swapped prefix would put equal elements out of order. **Debug builds.** Unoptimized, a network is many times the code of an insertion sort and no faster: a debug program that sorts integers was 804 KB with the networks and is 145 KB without, against 140 before, so `calx_sort.h` leaves them out without `__OPTIMIZE__`. A release build's `calx_sort.c` takes 0.74 s to compile, against 0.34, once, as it's cached. **Why**: against Rust 1.91 on the reference machine, in programs that sort a copy of a list just made, 1M random `i64` take 6.1 ms against `sort`'s 15.2 and `sort_unstable`'s 10.2 (D96: 8.6), 10k random 0.039 against 0.097 and 0.068 (D96: 0.066, behind `sort_unstable`), 1k 0.0026 against 0.0080 and 0.0053, 10,000 lists of 16 0.13 against 0.99 and 1.03, 1M in reverse 0.39 against 0.82 and 0.78 (D96: 0.80), 1M `i32` 3.9 against 12.6 and 9.6. Powers of two, a million below 2^62 plus 0 to 6, are the exception, 4.9 against `sort_unstable`'s 4.4: most fall in the lowest bucket, so they're quicksorted, as Rust's are, after the pass that finds which bits differ, 3 to 6% of the time. The quicksort is otherwise even with Rust's: 8 instructions an element in the partition, and 9.1 elements partitioned per element of the list, with no heapsort. Tried and dropped: radix sorting a quicksort's long runs again, which was slower, or only counting them, no faster; sampling for a crowded bucket before the pass that finds the differing bits, to skip it, within noise; a pivot from medians of medians taken recursively; and a majority vote in place of the sample, slower. `tests/lists/sort_ints.calx` checks every integer type, at the lengths and shapes where the algorithm changes, against the stable sort, which has none of this, and caught a wrong pad and a missing middle swap put in on purpose; a C stress test of 27 shapes ran clean in release, at `-O0`, and under ASan and UBSan | Phase 4 |
| D158 | 2026-10-29 | Lists grow by a cold call given copies (CLX-35) | A list's `push` and `insert`, a deque's pushes and a priority queue's check for room inline, as `len >= cap`, and grow by calling `cx_grow_to` or `cx_deque_grow_to`, out of line, cold and never inlined, given a copy of the capacity (and of a deque's head) in a local of its own. They used to be given the fields' addresses. A list in a local whose field's address goes to a call stays in memory, and as far as clang can tell a store to an element might change it, since a byte might be anything and an `i64` has the length's type. So after every store its pointer, length and capacity were read back. Given copies, the list stays in registers. Tried first and dropped: growing inline, all but the reallocation, which kept the list in registers too, but its arithmetic in every loop that pushes took registers from the loop and, in some loops, kept clang from counting pushes with the loop's counter, a third slower on a loop of bytes; and returning the buffer and capacity as a struct, which goes through memory on Windows and made the tick benchmark 1% slower. `restrict` on owned pointers, where the card started, does nothing here: clang honours it on parameters, which views already have. Measured on an i9-13900K pinned to one P-core, best of 50 (`bench/push`): a million values pushed onto a new list in a local, 4 bytes each, 1.01 → 0.83 ms; a byte each, 0.39 → 0.21; an `i64` each, 0.67 → 0.53; Rust's `Vec` 2.46, 0.53 and 2.35. The tick benchmark was 1% faster, 17,796 against 17,973 µs a tick over 10 pairs run in turn. A function given a list to change, when it isn't inlined, still keeps the length in memory, as Rust's `&mut Vec` does: CLX-53. | Phase 4 |
| D159 | 2026-10-29 | A function with a loop works on a copy of a `mut` list (CLX-53) | A function with a loop that's given a list or a deque to change, or a struct that holds one, copies it into a local as it starts, works on the copy and writes it back at each way out: falling off its end, `return`, `fail` and `try`. Through the view, clang reads the length and capacity back after every store of a byte or an `i64`, `restrict` or not, since the pointer it stores through was loaded from memory and might, as far as it can tell, point at the list itself. Nothing else can see the list until the function returns: views can't be stored or returned, `mut` views never overlap (D48), and a panic ends the program. A call given the view again is given the copy. Of a struct, the copy holds the fields the function names, or all of it if it's used whole, as a call's argument or a method's receiver, and only up to 8 scalars, copied one at a time. Copied as one value, an optional string the loop never touched stayed in memory and was copied again by each method inlined into the loop, which made the writer below twice as slow. A function without a loop uses the view, since the write-back is a store for each field that only a loop wins back. Measured on an i9-13900K pinned to one P-core, best of 50 (`bench/push`), a million values onto a list given to change, called through a function value clang can't see through: 4 bytes each, 1.70 → 0.79 ms; a byte each, 0.44 → 0.22; an `i64` each, 0.60 → 0.33; Rust's `&mut Vec` 1.71, 0.45 and 0.62. A writer, a struct of a list and an optional string, writing an int and a VarInt each through its methods: 1.67 → 1.15 ms, Rust 2.57. The tick benchmark was about 2% slower, 18,490 against 18,146 µs a tick over 3 pairs run in turn, as it was with every function copying; not traced, and accepted for the encoders' gain. | Phase 4 |
| D160 | 2026-10-29 | String pushes are inline (CLX-54) | `s.push(r)` and `s.push_str(t)` compile to inline C: a check for room, then a store or a copy. Growing is `cx_string_grow_to`, out of line and cold, given a copy of the capacity, as a list's is (D158). They used to be calls given the string's address, so a string in a local stayed in memory and every rune cost a call. A literal's string has a capacity of 0, so the check is `len + n > cap`, which sends its first push to the call, where the literal's bytes are copied; `cap - len` would wrap. A rune that isn't ASCII, with 4 bytes of room, is written with no branch on its length: all 4 bytes go in, and the length counts the 2 to 4 that are its own, since in mixed text a branch on the length guesses wrong often, as Rust's `push` does. D159's copy of a `mut` view now covers a `String`, and a struct that holds one. Measured on an i9-13900K pinned to one P-core, best of 50 (`bench/strings`): a million ASCII runes onto a new string, 1.69 → 0.54 ms, Rust 0.67; runes of 1 to 4 bytes mixed, 7.16 → 2.86, Rust 6.22; words of 3 to 7 bytes, 7.28 → 6.51, Rust 8.23; ASCII runes onto a string given to change, not inlined, 1.64 → 0.48, Rust 0.57; a text escaped as JSON, 2.94 → 1.94, Rust 2.06. The tick benchmark, which builds no strings in its loop, was unchanged: 18,386 against 18,521 µs a tick over 2 pairs. | Phase 4 |
| D161 | 2026-10-29 | The protocol suites, and who may join (P4.8, CAL-42) | The protocol bot (`src/rig/session.calx`), one player made from Calcite's protocol code, runs five scenarios, `status`, `outdated`, `bad-name`, `stranger` and `join`, against vanilla 26.3 and against Calcite, with the same `server.properties` and a `whitelist.json` that lists it, in two configurations, compressed and not. It writes down every packet each server sends, the vanilla adapter's `SessionDump` reads them with vanilla's codecs, and `rig/runner/sessiondiff.py` lines each step's packets up by kind. Two packets match when their values and bytes are the same, except: the clock's packets are compared once a step without their times; a step's chunks are compared as a set, by position; where vanilla itself writes a value in other bytes each run (tags, `set_time`, a chunk's heightmaps), only the value counts; and the session ID and an unsigned chat message's time, which vanilla draws each run, are left out. A difference is keyed `session:SCENARIO/STEP/KIND`, which a file in `rig/differences/` can cover; status, login and configuration gate (G4.2), and play is reported. Vanilla runs only on a developer's machine, from the jar's classes, with Mojang's EULA accepted in the rig's scratch folder under `build/rig/sessions/` (Elliot's approval), never in CI and never with its jar or code committed. The suites found that 26.3's `white-list` defaults to true, so Calcite now checks logins as vanilla's `canPlayerLogin` does, in its order: bans, the whitelist (or an op), IP bans, then `max-players`, with the op's `bypassesPlayerLimit`. It reads vanilla's four lists from its folder as it starts, writes `[]` to each that's missing, and logs a list it can't read without writing over it. A ban that has ended is no ban, where vanilla throws once on it. The brand, `calcite`, is the first `identity` difference: kept, since it's how a client or a server list tells what it's talking to (D132). The lists are read once, until the commands that change them (P8.5) | Phase 4 |
| D162 | 2026-10-29 | Vanilla's client checks the signed chat, and the game itself joins (G4.1) | The signed-chat test passed against Calcite's own idea of signatures, which a wrong idea would pass on both ends, as the light masks did (D145). So it now writes what each of its three clients is sent to a file of its own in `build/packets/chat/`, with the test's Mojang public key, and the vanilla adapter's `ChatCheck` reads each file as vanilla's client reads chat: a player's chat session must pass `RemoteChatSession.Data.validate` against that key, each `player_chat` must carry the next global index, its last-seen list is unpacked with vanilla's `MessageSignatureCache`, and its signature must pass the sender's `SignedMessageValidator` with the link chain. A key's expiry is judged at each message's own time, since the test's clock is fixed in 2025. All 11 messages pass, 9 of them signed, including the full signatures a client that joined late is sent, so Calcite's signed payloads are vanilla's. Then `rig/runner/realclient.py` runs Mojang's own 26.3 client, from a launcher's files checked against the version file's SHA-1, against Calcite's release build offline: it joins, walks about 11 blocks, jumps and chats, and Calcite logs the join and the message, which comes back to the client, with no disconnect and no error but the offline account's. Keys go to the client's window only while it's in front. Nothing of Mojang's is copied: the client runs from the launcher's files, on a developer's machine, never in CI. With `--online NAME`, it plays as the Modrinth App's account NAME, the player's own (Elliot's leave): it renews the session as the launcher does, from the launcher's Microsoft refresh token through Xbox Live to a Minecraft access token, in memory, never printed or written, and nothing goes back to the launcher. Calcite runs in online mode, and the message comes through signed on both sides, so G4.1 passes: Ellipog joined, walked and said `signed chat from a real client`, which Calcite and the client both logged without `[Not Secure]`. Why: the protocol is only proven against the game, and the game's own chat checks are the ones a player meets | Phase 4 |
| D163 | 2026-10-29 | std.palette: vanilla's paletted containers, in std (P5.1, CLX-5) | A chunk section's block states and biomes are `std.palette`'s `PalettedContainer`, on a `BitStorage` that packs values as `SimpleBitStorage` does, under a `Strategy` for block states (axis 4; 4 bits as a list up to 16 values, 5 to 8 with a hash up to 256, then the registry's IDs) or biomes (axis 2; 1 to 3 bits, then IDs). It's in std, not Calcite, because the bytes depend on the whole of vanilla's algorithm, each resize, which entries are dropped and in what order they're re-added, and a library others can build worlds with should have it right once. Growing, `write`, `pack` and `unpack` follow 26.3 exactly: a resize copies the values in index order into an empty palette, dropping entries nothing refers to; `pack` lists the values in the order they first appear and stores them in the bits a palette of that size takes on disk; `unpack` keeps a palette's longs as they are, padding bits too, and turns indices into IDs past the lists. The rig's `palette` probe checks it against vanilla's own classes: 129 cases, every one the same bytes. Where it differs, on purpose: an empty palette and an index past a palette's end fail `unpack` (`Error.Empty`, `Error.Index`), where vanilla loads them and fails later, when the value is read; a palette with an entry twice finds the first, where vanilla's hash palette finds one by identity hash; a 32-bit storage holds every `u32`, where vanilla's `int` check stops at 2^31 - 1; a storage holds at most 2^26 values, where the multiply-and-shift division stays exact. Reading a container from the network isn't there: the server only writes them. The palette is a list scanned in order up to 16 entries, and above that a list with an open-addressing hash of `u16` slots, twice the palette's room, by Fibonacci hashing, so finding a value never allocates. | Phase 5 |
| D164 | 2026-10-29 | Chunks as vanilla holds them, on block flags compiled in (P5.1, CAL-60) | `world.chunk` holds a chunk as `LevelChunk` does: a `Section` for each 16 blocks of height, with `std.palette`'s containers for its states and biomes and vanilla's four counts (blocks that aren't air, those that take random ticks, those holding a fluid, and those whose fluid takes random ticks), and the six heightmaps, in `BitStorage`s of as many bits as the height needs. `set_block` follows `LevelChunk.setBlockState`: nothing changes for air into a section of only air or for the state already there, and then `MOTION_BLOCKING`, `MOTION_BLOCKING_NO_LEAVES`, `OCEAN_FLOOR` and `WORLD_SURFACE`, in that order, are told of it as `Heightmap.update` tells them, scanning down when the top block goes. `prime_heightmaps` follows `primeHeightmaps`, from the top of the highest section that isn't empty, down to the first state each heightmap counts. What a state is comes from vanilla's state cache: the vanilla adapter's `BlockFlags` writes each state's air, random ticks, fluid and the fluid's ticks, skylight, occlusion, solid render, block entity, light emission and dampening to `generated/26.3/block_flags.json`, and `calx_tables.py` compiles them into `world.block_flags`, as strings with a printable character for each state, an index into the 102 different values, commonest first, the 13 rarest as two. They're decoded into a list once as the server starts.
