Which heightmaps count a state isn't in the cache in 26.3: the two `OCEAN_FLOOR` ones and `MOTION_BLOCKING` take the data pack's `blocks_motion_in_heightmap` tag, and `MOTION_BLOCKING_NO_LEAVES` its `_no_leaves` tag, as the server loads them, so a data pack that changes the tags changes the heightmaps; both `MOTION_BLOCKING` ones count fluids too, and the `WORLD_SURFACE` ones every state that isn't air. The flat world is now built through it, layer by layer as `FlatLevelSource` builds one, and sends the same 7,260 bytes it did. The rig's `chunk` probe checks it against vanilla's own `LevelChunkSection` and `Heightmap` in a `ProtoChunk`, with blocks set as `LevelChunk` sets them: 13 cases, from one section high to the tallest a dimension can be, all the same. Where it differs, on purpose: the packet's heightmaps go by kind, 1, 4 and 5, where vanilla's order comes from a `HashMap` of identity hashes and changes from run to run (D161); and `ProtoChunk`'s own `setBlockState`, which tells only its status's heightmaps and checks `is(AIR)` rather than `isAir`, waits for generation (P5.2). Why: every later part of Phase 5 reads and writes blocks through this, and it's cheaper to match vanilla's counts and heightmaps once, against vanilla, than to find each difference in a client | Phase 5 |
| D165 | 2026-10-29 | Parts of files written in place, without handles (CLX-55) | `std.fs` writes parts of a file in place with `write_parts(path, parts, sync)`: each `(offset, bytes)` in order, creating the file if it's missing and keeping the rest, then, with `sync`, waiting until the system says it's on the disk, as Java's `FileChannel.force(true)`. `read_parts(path, ranges)` reads many ranges of a file. Each opens the file once, does all it was given, and closes it. Why not a `File` handle kept open, as vanilla keeps its region files open, 256 at most: Calx has no destructors, so a handle a program forgot, or dropped on an error path, would stay open, the one leak ownership couldn't catch. Batches cost about what a handle does where it matters: a region's chunks are saved together, one opening for all of them and one for the header. The sync is what lets region files be crash-safe: the chunks go into sectors nothing points at, are synced, and only then is the header that points at them written, so a crash leaves the old header and the old chunks, or the new header and chunks that are all there | Phase 5 |
| D166 | 2026-10-29 | Region files: vanilla's layout, written in batches (P5.2, CAL-61) | `storage.region` keeps a region file's header in memory and opens the file only to read or write parts of it (D165), so nothing stays open. A chunk goes where vanilla's `RegionFile` puts it: into the first free sectors that fit, found while its old ones are still taken, or, at 256 sectors or more, into `c.X.Z.mcc` beside it, with a stub in one sector. A write takes a batch of chunks, encoded beforehand, on any thread: their bytes go in one call, then the header, and their old sectors are freed only after the whole batch, so no chunk lands on another's old bytes while the header on the disk still points at them. One chunk to a batch is vanilla's order, and gives its file byte for byte, which the rig's `region` probe checks. With `sync` (`sync-chunk-writes`), the chunks are on the disk before the header that points at them is written. Unlike vanilla, a write pads the file to whole sectors as it goes, not only when it's closed, and a chunk's own file is moved into place before the header, not after: a header that still points at the old place doesn't read it, unless the old chunk had its own file too, in which case the new bytes are read, whole. | Phase 5 |
| D167 | 2026-10-29 | A file lock, taken as Java takes one (CLX-56) | `std.fs.try_lock(path, bytes)` does what vanilla's `DirectoryLock` does with a `FileChannel`: it opens the file to write, creating it and keeping what it holds, writes the bytes at its start, syncs them, then takes an exclusive lock on the whole file without waiting, and gives `none` if one's held. On Windows it's `LockFileEx` over Java's range (0 to `0x7FFFFFFFFFFFFFFF`) on a handle shared to read and write, as Java opens it, so a Calx server and vanilla refuse each other; because that lock stops another program's writes too, a write refused by another's lock is `none` as well, as Java's own write fails there. On Linux it's an open file description lock (`F_OFD_SETLK`, `F_SETLK` where the kernel lacks it), which conflicts with the `fcntl` locks Java takes and, unlike them, isn't let go when the program closes another handle to the file. A `FileLock` is held until `release` or the program's end, since Calx has no destructors; it's the one handle `std.fs` leaves open (D165). Checked both ways against Java's `FileChannel.tryLock` on both systems | Phase 5 |
| D168 | 2026-10-30 | The world folder as vanilla keeps it, and no upgrading (P5.2, CAL-61) | `storage.world` opens a world's folder as `LevelStorageSource` does: made if it's missing, locked with `session.lock` (D167), `level.dat` read with the `level.dat_old` fallback and a broken one kept as `level.dat_corrupted_` and the local time, and saved to a new file moved over it by `safe_replace`, vanilla's `Util.safeReplaceOrMoveFile` with its 10 tries and every line it logs. A world's version is checked as `Main` checks it (`storage.level.openable`). Calcite has no DataFixerUpper, so it refuses a world whose `DataVersion` is older than 26.3's, 5023, and says to open it once with a vanilla 26.3 server and `--forceUpgrade`; a newer one loads, as vanilla loads it. Files are gzipped by std.zlib, whose deflate isn't zlib's, so a saved `level.dat` holds the same NBT in other bytes, which the `world_folder` probe compares by what they inflate to. Vanilla's check for symbolic links in the folder waits for `std.fs` to read links | Phase 5 |
| D169 | 2026-10-31 | Saved data as vanilla keeps it, and older files read as they are (P5.2, CAL-61) | `storage.saved` keeps a `data` folder, the world's or a dimension's, as `SavedDataStorage` does: each type's `<namespace>/<path>.dat` holds its `data` and the `DataVersion` it was saved in, is read gzipped or plain as its first two bytes say, with no quota, and is written gzipped straight over the old file, as vanilla writes it, with vanilla's three lines. What's in `data` is each type's to read, with its own codec, as its feature arrives. D168 refuses a world older than 26.3, but vanilla's `--forceUpgrade` upgrades only chunks, entities and POI, and vanilla upgrades a player's data, advancements and stats as they join, and saved data as it's read, which it saves only once it changes. So a world D168 lets in can still hold files saved in older versions: those of players who haven't joined since, and saved data that hasn't changed. Calcite reads such a file as it is and logs a warning naming it and its data version (`storage.level.older_file`), since refusing it would lose a player's data or the scoreboard on the next save, and stopping the server for one would keep a world out that vanilla opens. Upgrading them is [CAL-74](https://acta.aaenz.no/aaen-studios/calcite#CAL-74) | Phase 5 |
| D170 | 2026-10-31 | Advancements and statistics as files now, their contents with their features (P5.2, CAL-61) | `storage.progress` reads and writes `players/advancements` and `players/stats` as vanilla's `PlayerAdvancements` and `ServerStatsCounter` do around their codecs: the file read through `Files.newBufferedReader` (`java.reader`) with `StrictJsonParser` (`json.strict`), each kind's lines for one it can't read, the data version its codec would read, and the save written with their pretty `GSON` (`json.gson`) and 26.3's `DataVersion`; and statistics under a player's name moved to their UUID's as `locateStatsFile` moves them. What a file holds is for the advancements' and statistics' codecs to read, which come with them (P6.5). As D169 has it, a file saved in an older version, or with no `DataVersion`, which vanilla reads as 1343, is read as it is with `older_file`'s warning, and so is one whose `DataVersion` vanilla's `intValue` throws for, such as `1e10000`, which vanilla doesn't catch, with a warning of its own. A `\u` escape of half a surrogate pair is read as U+FFFD, since a Calx `String` is UTF-8, where Java keeps the half: the one place Calcite's reading differs from Gson's. Pretty writing and the `int` of a number were checked against Gson 2.14.0 on 190 cases | Phase 5 |
| D171 | 2026-10-31 | Entity and POI chunks as vanilla keeps them, their NBT now (P5.2, CAL-61) | `storage.entities` reads an entity chunk as `EntityStorage.loadEntities` does before it makes the entities: `Position` read with `ChunkPos.CODEC`'s lines, a chunk elsewhere or with no position read anyway, and `Entities` read element by element through `storage.problems`, vanilla's `ProblemReporter` tree, logged as one `Serialization errors:` warning on `EntityStorage`'s logger; and saves one as `storeEntities` does, nothing for a chunk with no entities. `storage.poi` reads and writes a POI chunk as `SectionStorage` does with `PoiSection`'s codec: sections read one by one, the lenient ones kept, records by type name through `RegistryFixedCodec`'s messages, a record of another type at the same place taking the first's with vanilla's error, and records saved in `Short2ObjectOpenHashMap`'s order. As D169 has it, an older chunk is read as it is with `older_file`'s warning. This is the NBT layer: finding chunks in their region files, the set of chunks known to have no entities, the `IOWorker`, the `Failed to load` and `Failed to store` lines and `reportChunkLoadFailure`'s reports come with the server's world (P5.2, CAL-61). Checked on 147 `entities_poi` cases | Phase 5 |
| D172 | 2026-10-31 | What a world's round trip through Calcite must keep (G5.2, CAL-61) | A world vanilla made and saved, loaded and saved by Calcite, must be the same world to vanilla: every file read with vanilla's own readers (`RegionFile`, `NbtIo`, Gson) the same tree as vanilla's, chunk by chunk, with nothing logged reading it, and vanilla's server opening it with no line it doesn't log opening its own and answering the same questions about what's in it. What a save can't keep is left out: `level.dat`'s `LastPlayed`, the region files' timestamps, where chunks sit in them and their compressed bytes; and a backup holds the file it replaced. `rig/runner/roundtrip.py` checks it, with `rig.resave` as Calcite's half, which goes through the `storage` modules; once the server loads and saves worlds itself, the round trip goes through the server, and must still pass. It needs vanilla's server, so it isn't in CI | Phase 5 |
| D173 | 2026-10-31 | Keys taken out of fastutil tables as its iterator takes them (CLX-59, CAL-64) | `OpenHashMap.remove_marked(marks)` and `OpenHashSet.remove_marked(marks)` take out the keys a loop marked, one mark for each key in the loop's order, as a loop over fastutil's table that calls its iterator's `remove` on them does, and give the order that loop goes over the keys in, as places in the marking loop. Taking a key out moves the keys after it in its run back; one that moves from the first slots to the last is gone over after the rest, found again by its hash, and taken out as `remove` does, which is the only place the loop halves the table. Vanilla's loops that remove with the iterator, `TicketStorage.removeTicketIf` and `ChunkMap.processUnloads`, act on each key as they go, so Calcite marks the keys, then does what vanilla does for each in the order given. Marks are all made first, which fits, since those loops decide each key by the key and its value alone. Checked against fastutil 8.5.18 in the `collections` probe: 7,698 loops in 420 cases, 183 of them out of the table's order and 17 halving it, all the same on Windows and Linux | Calx, Phase 5 |
| D174 | 2026-10-31 | The Calx website now, in the calx repo (CLX-60) | Elliot wants the Calx website built now rather than in Phase 9, so it has its own track on the calx board, the Website project, outside the phases. It lives in `calx/site/` rather than a repo of its own: it renders `calx/docs/` unchanged (D40), so a language change and its docs land in one commit, the site always shows what the compiler at that commit does, and the docs' examples stay tests against that compiler (D41). A push that only changes `site/` skips the compiler's CI, and `site/` doesn't count toward the repo's languages on GitHub. P9.4 keeps the plugin guide and the host API reference. Calcite's own site waits | Calx, Website |
| D175 | 2026-10-31 | Chunk tickets tell a generic listener, not two set on them (CAL-64) | `TicketStorage`'s changers take a `TicketListener`, a generic with `loading` and `simulation`, where vanilla's storage holds two `LongIntBooleanConsumer`s, set once, that call the loading and simulation trackers. Each call is made where vanilla makes it, in the same order, so the trackers are told the same things in the same sequence. Vanilla's listeners are the trackers' `update`, which checks an edge from no source and so doesn't read the storage back while it's being changed; the tracker can be passed in beside the storage instead of kept in it, which Calx's borrows need, and nothing is lost. `purgeStaleTickets`, which asks the chunk map whether a chunk's save has finished, asks a generic `Holders` the same way. A generic is compiled for each listener, so there's no indirect call and nothing allocated. Checked in the `tickets` probe: 274 cases, every call, order and byte, on Windows and Linux | Phase 5 |
| D176 | 2026-10-31 | Chunk levels are a graph given its levels and sources as it works (CAL-64) | Vanilla's trackers are each a subclass of `ChunkTracker`, keeping their levels and reading their sources through fields. Calcite's `world.levels` is one `ChunkTracker` struct, the queue and the levels being worked out, and each tracker's levels are a type that implements `ChunkLevels`, `level` and `set_level`, and `LevelSources<S>`, which reads a chunk's source from an `S`: the loading and simulation levels read a dimension's `TicketStorage`, each its own way, and the player trackers read who's in each chunk. `update` takes the levels alone, since vanilla's never reads a source, so a `TicketListener` can call it while the tickets are being changed; `run_updates` takes both. The graph reads a chunk's source only when its neighbours don't already give it 0, where vanilla reads it as it comes to it, which gives the same level, since a level is clamped to 0. Checked in the `levels` probe: 270 cases, every level set and its order, on Windows and Linux | Phase 5 |
| D177 | 2026-10-31 | The distance manager's threads are queues it's told to run (CAL-64) | Vanilla's `DistanceManager` hands its player ticket dispatcher's jobs to a worker thread and their tasks to the main thread, so when each runs is up to the threads, and it updates the holders whose level changed in the order of `chunksToUpdateFutures`, a set of their identities whose order is different every run. Calcite's `world.distance` keeps the dispatcher's jobs in vanilla's four priorities and the main thread's tasks in a line, and runs one when it's told to, `run_dispatcher` and `run_main`, so the same calls give the same tickets every time; the server is to run them right after each update, as vanilla's worker runs its jobs as soon as it can. It updates the holders in the order their levels changed, a `LinkedSet`. A holder's future of its chunk ticking its entities is where it is, `Ticking`, with a count of the releases waiting on it, and a holder is told from one made since by a serial, as vanilla tells them apart by identity. The vanilla adapter runs vanilla's two executors as queues the case runs and swaps the set of identities for a `LinkedHashSet`, so vanilla runs the same way every time too. Checked in the `distance` probe: 102 cases of players joining, leaving and walking, tickets, view and simulation distances and holders dropping and unloading, with the jobs and tasks run in random interleavings, on Windows and Linux | Phase 5 |
| D178 | 2026-10-31 | Chunks are read on threads, and what's read is the server's input (CAL-65) | Vanilla reads a chunk's region file on its IO worker, parses it on its background executor and makes it loaded on the main thread as the futures complete, so what loads when is up to the threads. Calcite's `server.chunk_jobs` has one IO thread own the region files, finding which chunks a region's file has and reading a chunk's sectors, and chunk workers, up to one fewer than the processors and at most 8, that inflate and parse what it read into a chunk ready to send. Each wakes the poll as it's done. The tick thread takes what came back in the order it came, records the IO thread's part, the sectors it read and the lines it logged, in the input log, now version 2, makes the chunk loaded, and runs the distance manager until it settles. What a worker makes depends only on what was read, so a replay runs `prepare` again from the log, with no files, and has the same chunks at the same points. Whether the world is read is recorded too: a server that can't wake its poll has no threads to read with, and generates every chunk flat, live and replayed. A holder that unloads while its chunk is read keeps the chunk's place until the read is back, then lets it go, or keeps it if a holder came back for it. Vanilla keeps the holder itself until then, through its save future, which comes to the same chunks. An unsaved chunk is the flat world's one body, with no `Chunk` of its own; a saved one is sent with the light it saved, without its block entities (CAL-66, Phase 6). Measured on a vanilla world of 4 regions: a view of 10 joins with 473 chunks, 302 of them different; walking at 40 blocks a second sends 621 more and forgets 621, none sent twice; a recorded walk replays with 1,417 packets and lines compared and 0 different | Phase 5 |
| D179 | 2026-10-31 | A view past the tickets' reach reads its far chunks for each player (CAL-65) | Vanilla's views stop at 32, and a player's tickets make every chunk of the view ready. `force-view-distance` goes to 1,024 (D155), where tickets would load millions of holders and run the distance manager over all of them, so the tickets stop where vanilla's do, at 32. A chunk more than 33 from the player on an axis is read for the player when their sender comes to it: its region's file is asked first which chunks it has, so those it hasn't are sent flat at once, and the rest are read, at most 64 for each player at once, with the sender stopping there until some come back. Each is let go once it's sent, when it leaves the player's view, or when they leave. Two players who need the same far chunk each read it, since sharing it would need the holders the tickets give. Measured: views of 32 and 64, walking at 40 blocks a second, sent 4,864 and 11,896 chunks, none twice | Phase 5 |
| D180 | 2026-10-31 | The Calx site speaks to readers outside the project (CLX-63) | calxlang.com is read by people who don't have Calcite's plans beside them. So the site leaves out the decision ids the docs cite, and the docs cite one only in parentheses at the end of the sentence it supports, never in a heading or as a word in the sentence, so every sentence reads the same without it. The reference and the std pages describe each feature for programs in general: Minecraft comes up where a module exists for it, such as `std.palette` and `std.lz4`, or as one example among others, and "vanilla" and Calcite's own needs stay in the spec. Java's and fastutil's hash maps get a sidebar group of their own, Java compatibility, since most programs want `Map`. What's planned is on a roadmap page, `docs/roadmap.md`, not only in the spec. The site says of Calcite only what's built and what it aims at, and calls nothing measured until it is. The home page drops the template look: no pill, kickers, stat strip or icon grid, a serif for headings and the system's font for the rest | Calx, Website |
| D181 | 2026-10-31 | Light is read as the last run left it, keeping only what changed (CAL-66) | Vanilla's `LayerLightSectionStorage` lights one map of layers, `updatingSectionData`, and at the end of each run that changed a section, `swapSectionMap` copies it into `visibleSectionData`, which `getLightValue` and `getDataLayerData` read, with the sky's top sections and lowest section as they were then. A layer is copied on its first change after a swap, so the copy is never changed. Calcite's `world.light` keeps one map, and beside it what's changed since the last run ended: each section, with its layer as the run left it, and each column whose top changed, with its old top, all dropped when a run ends. Reading light looks there first. So a run copies only the sections it changes, as vanilla's copy on write does, and no map. A section lit from the light queued for it takes vanilla's queued `DataLayer` itself, which the queue keeps until the end of the next run, so what's set in it meanwhile, by the sky's `setLightEnabled` or `propagateLightSources`, shows in what's queued. Calcite marks such a section shared and reads its queued light from the layer it's lit with. The rig's `light` probe checks it, with cases that read between every kind of change. | Phase 5 |
| D182 | 2026-10-31 | Sections count the blocks that give light, and the sky's light is set a level at a time (CAL-66) | Vanilla's `findBlockLightSources` skips a section whose palette has no state that gives light and looks through every block of the rest. Calcite's sections count the blocks that give light, as they count those that tick, so light skips a section with none, even one whose palette still names such a state. `propagateLightSources` reads the sky sources of the chunk's columns and of its neighbours' edges once, takes each section's layer out of the map once, and sets whole levels of a section to 15 where every column's sources go below them, then queues the same light in vanilla's order. Measured with rig/runner/light_timing.py, the `light` probe's time less its time with the light left out, medians of 15 on Windows: 81 flat chunks in 1.38 ms against vanilla's 3.94 ms, 2.9 times as fast; 49 chunks of random terrain in 168 ms against 258 ms, 1.5 times; and those with 5,000 blocks changed in 201 ms against 330 ms, 1.6 times, all with the same light. Before these, the flat chunks took 5.65 ms, half vanilla's speed. | Phase 5 |
| D183 | 2026-10-31 | The flat world's light comes from the light engines (CAL-66) | The flat world's chunk is built once and sent everywhere, with its light. That light is now `world.light`'s: the chunk lit as vanilla lights a chunk, with the same chunk standing in for every neighbour, so its edges are lit as in the middle of a flat world. [`test/light.calx`](../src/test/light.calx) checks it against the middle chunk of three by three lit together. Each chunk of the level lit as vanilla lights it, saved ones with their light and flat ones beside others, is CAL-76. | Phase 5 |
| D184 | 2026-10-31 | A chunk's light is sent with it, and light updates wait for block changes (CAL-76) | Vanilla lights a chunk once the 3×3 around it is initialized, and sends a player a chunk once it ticks, when the chunks around it are lit. Light spreads 15 blocks at most, less than a chunk is wide, so lighting a chunk two away can't change a sent chunk's light: it's final until a block changes. Vanilla still sends `light_update`s as it lights, since `markSectionAndNeighborsAsAffected` marks the sections next to every one that changed, and so the chunks at the edge of what's lit get updates though their light is the same. On the round trip's saved world (`sessions.py --config saved`), vanilla's edge updates carry the light its chunk packets did, and its others come with block changes, as water flows in caves and the round trip's sand falls. Calcite lights the level's chunks in vanilla's order, with `world.light`'s engines, and sends each with the engine's light and no updates after it: a client ends with the same light, and updates come with block changes (Phase 6). 73 of the saved world's 81 chunks match vanilla's byte for byte, light included; the rest differ by block entities and block ticks (Phase 6). On a world vanilla saved full from -16 to 15, a player at a view of 12 gets the last of its 637 chunks in a median 698 ms, against 682 ms before, as the client's batches pace it. A replay of that join, the tick thread's work without the network, takes 243 ms against 198: 21 ms for the ring of 120 chunks around the view, which vanilla reads too, for their light; 18 ms on the workers for each chunk's sky sources, 19 µs a chunk, which is vanilla's `ChunkSkyLightSources.fillFrom`, a walk down each column to the first edge that stops light, kept as it is; and 16 ms of light on the tick thread, 7 of them initializing chunks and 8 putting the engine's light in packets. Boot is unchanged, 1.5 ms to the port. | Phase 5 |
| D185 | 2026-10-31 | New players' spawns are found as vanilla finds them, and the world's files read through the link (CAL-75) | Vanilla's `PrepareSpawnTask` reads where a player was from their data, or searches for a place near the world's spawn: candidates in the square `respawn_radius` reaches, taken in a walk that starts at random, each checked once a `spawn_search` ticket has its chunk full, the first a player can stand on and fits in, or else the spawn moved to where they fit. Calcite does the same at the configuration ticks, through the level's tickets, so the player joins on the same block, and the spawn ticket loads the same chunks. The walk's start is drawn from the link's random bytes, into a `LegacyRandom` for `nextInt`, where vanilla's comes from a thread-local random, which can't be matched anyway; the rig makes `respawn_radius` 0 on both servers. Collisions use each state's collision shape, generated from vanilla's with the flags `COLLISION_TOP_FULL` and `LARGE_COLLISION`, so a block's top and its reach out of its block are table lookups and only the shapes that need it are checked box by box. `level.dat`, the game rules and each joining player's data are now read through the link's `FILE` record, which the input log keeps, so a replay sees what the recording read without the world's folder; the log's version is 3. Vanilla's warnings for a saved place it can't fully read aren't logged yet, and a saved place in another dimension is put in the overworld until there are others (`P5.10`). | Phase 5 |
| D186 | 2026-10-31 | A world's symbolic links are checked as vanilla checks them, walked in sorted order (CAL-73) | Before it opens a world, Calcite reads `allowed_symlinks.txt` as vanilla's `PathAllowList` reads it and walks the world's folder for links it doesn't allow, failing with vanilla's `ContentValidationException` and its message, as `storage.symlinks`. Vanilla walks names in the order the file system lists them, which Calcite doesn't get from every system, so it walks them sorted: as Windows lists them, with ASCII letters uppercased, and elsewhere by their bytes, which is the order of no file system in particular but the same on every one. Only the message's order can differ, so the rig's cases have one forbidden link at most. Targets are matched as `Files.readSymbolicLink` gives them, as vanilla matches them, not resolved or normalized; a world that's a link is walked from its target as it's written, from the folder the server runs in; a Windows junction is a folder, as Java takes it; and the world's folder is made where it really is, as `FileUtil.createDirectoriesSafe` makes it, so a world that's a link to a file fails naming the file. The walk costs a look at each entry of the world's folder, not its files, as the server starts. The rig's cases that make links run on Linux, since Windows lets few users make them, and say so with a `system` field, which `record` and `score` honour: on another system a case isn't run and scores as elsewhere, and its expected hash is kept. The `allow_list` probe asks vanilla's matcher about targets as each system writes them, with its own cases for Windows and Linux. | Phase 5 |
| D187 | 2026-10-31 | Java's regular expressions and globs, as far as `allowed_symlinks.txt` needs them, fail closed past that (CAL-73) | `[regex]` and `[glob]` lines are Java's, so `java.regex` compiles and matches patterns as `java.util.regex` does, with its messages, indexes and backtracking order, and `java.glob` turns globs into the patterns Java's file systems turn them into, both written from black-box tests of JDK 25 and checked against it on thousands of random cases. What Calcite doesn't do yet, Unicode properties (`\p`, `\P`), grapheme clusters (`\X`, `\b{g}`), `CANON_EQ` and `UNICODE_CHARACTER_CLASS`'s classes, fails to compile as `unsupported`, and an allow list with such a pattern refuses every link, with a line of Calcite's saying why, rather than allowing one vanilla might refuse. Java's `\b` takes a non-spacing mark after a letter or digit of any script as a word character, past other marks, a UTF-16 unit at a time, while `\w` is ASCII; Calcite does the same, from tables of the categories JDK 25 gives, which `oracle/unicode/categories.py` writes as `java.categories`. | Phase 5 |
| D188 | 2026-11-01 | Saves survive a power cut: synced files, synced moves, and no file written in place (CAL-62) | Vanilla syncs a player's data and `level.dat` before moving them into place, but not the move, and it writes saved data, advancements and statistics in place, so a crash part way through leaves them half written. Calcite writes every file it saves to a new file beside it, syncs it, and moves it over the old with `std.fs.rename_synced`, which returns once the move is on the disk: `MOVEFILE_WRITE_THROUGH` on Windows, and on Linux `rename` and then `fsync` on the folders it moved between. After a crash or a power cut each file is the old one or the new one, whole. `replace_file` does it for the files vanilla writes in place, as `<path>-<random>.tmp`, and opens the file to write first, as vanilla opens it, so a folder or a read-only file it can't write is left as it is, with vanilla's line, as vanilla leaves it. `safe_replace`'s moves are synced, and a region file's chunk of its own, with `sync-chunk-writes`, is synced and moved synced. Each save waits for the disk once more than vanilla's. A file replaced this way is a new file, so on Linux it loses hard links, and its owner and permissions are the program's, where an in-place write keeps the old file's; the rig's `world_folder` probe matches vanilla on Windows and Linux. | Phase 5 |
| D189 | 2026-11-01 | Players and `level.dat` are saved on a thread of their own, and what it logs is the server's input (CAL-62) | Vanilla saves a player's data as they leave, every player and the world every 6000 ticks, `max(100, tps * 300)`, and as it stops, all on the server thread, which waits for the disk each time. Calcite's tick thread makes what's to be saved where vanilla saves it, a player's data or `level.dat`'s `Data`, and hands it to one saver thread, `server.saving`, which writes each in the order it was handed over, through D188's synced files and moves, then hands back what it logged and wakes the poll. One thread keeps the order, so `level.dat_old` and a player's `.dat_old` are the save before, as vanilla's are. A join reads the player's data, so it waits until every save handed over is written; that's the only wait, and it's for a disk write only while one is in flight, which an autosave or a player who left a moment before makes rare. The stop hands over every player and `level.dat`, then the server waits for the saver before it ends and lets go of `session.lock`. What a save logged is the server's input, as the IO thread's reads are (D178): the tick thread takes it as the saver's wake comes, logs it, and the link records it as a `SAVED` record when it isn't empty, in the input log, now version 4, so a replay, which has no folder and writes nothing, logs it at the same point. Saves that log nothing aren't recorded, so a recording's size doesn't grow with its saves. A line like `Failed to save player data for Steve` is logged a turn of the loop or more after vanilla would, on the server's thread, where vanilla logs it in its save. A player's data is what was read as they joined, or for a new player the `DataVersion`, `UUID` and `playerGameType` vanilla writes, with `Pos`, `Rotation` and `Dimension` as they are now; their statistics and advancements aren't written, as nothing changes them yet (Phase 6). `level.dat` is written only for a world that had one, from what was read, with the game time, which now goes on from the `Time` it saved where it started at 0, the wall clock's `LastPlayed`, and the server's brand, `calcite`, added to `ServerBrands` with `WasModded` set, as `setModdedInfo` does for a brand that isn't `vanilla`. The stop logs `stopServer`'s lines: `Saving players`, `Saving worlds` and `Saving chunks for level 'ServerLevel[<name>]'/minecraft:overworld` for the one level there is; vanilla logs the End's and the Nether's too (`P5.10`). Chunks aren't saved yet: nothing changes a chunk that was read, and the flat chunks made where none was saved stand in for vanilla's terrain, so saving them would write flat land into a vanilla world. The chunks worldgen makes are saved, through the IO thread that owns the region files, with CAL-68. | Phase 5 |
| D190 | 2026-11-01 | A world's data packs are found and picked as vanilla's, and the boot cache is keyed by what they're read from (CAL-67) | Calcite finds the data packs as vanilla's `PackRepository` finds them: vanilla's own, its feature packs from Mojang's archive, and each folder with a `pack.mcmeta` and each `.zip` in the world's `datapacks` folder, checked against `allowed_symlinks.txt`. It picks the ones that load as `configurePackRepository` picks them, from `level.dat`, or for a new world from `initial-enabled-packs` and `initial-disabled-packs`, with `--safeMode`, the feature packs a world's flags force on, and vanilla's lines. `level.dat` then keeps what was picked. Where it differs: (1) A `pack.mcmeta` that can't be read gets vanilla's warning and the exception's class. Where vanilla's message is DataFixerUpper's own check, such as `PackFormat`'s, the message is vanilla's words; where it's Gson's or DataFixerUpper's account of where in the JSON it went wrong, it's Calcite's. (2) A filter pattern that `java.regex` can't match yet (D187), such as one with Unicode properties, leaves the filter out, with a warning that says so, rather than hiding the wrong files. (3) A file whose path can't be an identifier is warned of when its folder is listed, as vanilla warns of it. But Calcite lists only the folders it loads so far, so it says nothing of files in the others, such as recipes and functions, until it loads them. (4) A folder pack's files are found as `Files.find` finds them, without following links, though a Windows junction counts as a folder. Calcite's walks stop 256 folders deep, where Java's would go on until the stack ran out. (5) Mojang's archive is pinned, so its feature packs' metadata always reads, and Calcite has no lines for it. (6) A zip that won't open is logged as `java.util.zip.ZipException` with Calcite's own reason, which is Java's for an empty file (`zip file is empty`); one that can't be read off the disk is logged with the NIO exception Java's `Files` throws. (7) Only `--safeMode` itself turns safe mode on. joptsimple's abbreviations, such as `--safe`, don't. (8) A replay reads the `datapacks` folder as it is now and makes nothing, since a recording doesn't hold its packs. (9) The registries load one after another, so a broken pack's errors come in a fixed order, where vanilla's workers log them in whatever order they finish. `No existing world data, creating new world` is logged after them, on a worker, as vanilla logs it, but outside the cached lines. (10) Vanilla throws `Tried to force ..., but it was already enabled` for a feature pack it forces that's already selected. That can't happen, since a selected pack's features are never missing, so Calcite has nothing for it. The boot cache (D140) is now keyed by a fingerprint too, as well as the build and the data: a SHA-256 of the setup (the folder, whether it's made, safe mode, whether the world is new, the packs on and off, the features) and of each entry in `datapacks`, walked, with its name, kind, size and modification time. Nothing is cached for a folder with a link in it, one with an entry that can't be read, or one that's missing and about to be made, so those load every start. A pack changed without its size or time changing would read stale. That's accepted, since editors, copies and unzipping all set the time. | Phase 5 |
| D191 | 2026-11-01 | Registry contents come from vanilla's codecs described as data, read by one interpreter (CAL-48) | Refines D134. Rather than a typed reader and writer for each of the 32 synchronized registries, Calcite describes each registry's network codec as data, a `Codec` in a `Codecs` arena (`registry.codec`), and one interpreter reads a data pack's JSON with it as DataFixerUpper's `Codec.parse` reads it with `JsonOps`, giving the NBT vanilla's `NbtOps` writes: defaults left out, each number with its type, a list of one written as its value, fields in a `JavaHashMap` so the compound's order is Java's. Errors are DataFixerUpper's messages, with partial values, since `Codec.either` picks by them. Where the network codec keeps fewer fields than the registry's own, as the variants drop their spawn conditions, the description reads only the ones it keeps from the same JSON, which gives what vanilla's decode then encode gives. A description is a few lines per registry and covers both validation and sending, where typed code would be a type, a reader and a writer each. The rig's `registries` probe (v2) checks every entry's bytes against `packRegistries`. | Phase 5 |
| D192 | 2026-11-01 | A case can compare its blobs with each compound sorted by key, where vanilla's order changes from run to run (CAL-48) | A timeline's tracks, and a dimension type's and a biome's attributes, are filtered to the ones clients are sent with `Map.copyOf`, whose `ImmutableCollections.MapN` probes from a seed picked at random each time the JVM starts, over `EnvironmentAttribute` keys hashed by identity. `NbtOps` then puts them into a `CompoundTag`'s `HashMap` in that order, and two keys that share a bucket keep the order they went in. So two runs of vanilla send the same entries in a different order: recording `contents-timeline` twice gave two hashes, and Calcite's equalled one of them. A client reads a compound into a map, so the order means nothing to it. A case may say `"unordered": true`, and the runner then sorts each NBT compound in its blobs by key before hashing them, for every adapter, so only what's in them is compared. The `registries` contents cases for `timeline`, `dimension_type` and `worldgen/biome` use it; every other case still compares bytes. Calcite writes the order its `JavaHashMap` gives, which is one of the orders vanilla can. | Phase 5 |
| D193 | 2026-11-01 | While the synchronized registries load, the registries loaded with a server's resources aren't there, and loot isn't validated yet (CAL-48) | Vanilla loads the registries of predicates, loot tables, item modifiers, slot sources and context float and int providers with a server's reloadable resources, after the world's registries, so `RegistryOps` has no lookup for them while an enchantment's conditions and numbers are read. An entry of one can then only be given in full: an ID fails with `RegistryFileCodec`'s "Registry does not exist", `RegistryFixedCodec`'s "Can't access registry", or, for a tag, `HolderSetCodec`'s "Registry ... is not available in this context", and `registry.codec` gives those for the six registries. Enchantments are read with vanilla's whole loot and predicate codecs (`registry.loot`, `registry.predicates`, `registry.enchantments`), which completes the 32 synchronized registries: 161 of 161 `registries` cases pass. Two of vanilla's checks are left for later. A `storage` provider's NBT path is taken as it's written, not parsed as `NbtPathArgument` parses it ([CAL-78](https://acta.aaenz.no/aaen-studios/calcite#CAL-78)). And conditions and providers aren't checked against the parameters their context has, as `ValidationContext` checks them once the resources load ([CAL-79](https://acta.aaenz.no/aaen-studios/calcite#CAL-79)); vanilla's own packs pass both, so only a world's pack can tell the difference. An NBT predicate's SNBT syntax error is given in English, as `CommandSyntaxException.getMessage` gives it on a server. | Phase 5 |
| D194 | 2026-11-01 | The synchronized registries load from the world's packs as vanilla's do, read with their network codecs, and what stops them is logged as vanilla logs it (CAL-48) | `registry.load` loads what a client is sent from the picked packs, in `RegistryDataLoader.load`'s order: the built-in registries' tags, then each synchronized registry's entries, read with its codec for the network, of which only the ones that read are registered, then its tags. A file that isn't JSON, an entry its codec refuses, an entry or tag that's named and never comes, a registry of mob variants left empty and a time marker two timelines of a clock define stop the server, and are logged in one `Registry loading errors` line as `printFullDetailsToLog` logs them, by registry and element in vanilla's order, with each exception as `printStackTrace` prints it less its stack frames; Gson's are named without their messages. The server keeps each entry's contents in NBT, and sends them only for entries from a pack the client doesn't know, so a world's pack reaches a client, where before only vanilla's entries did. The `packs` probe's `load` checks this against vanilla's whole `RegistryDataLoader.load`, on 29 cases: new entries in several registries, vanilla's overridden, defaults given, numbers in other shapes, a world's tags, and 23 ways to stop it.Vanilla reads the world's registries with its codecs for the server, so two things are left for later ([CAL-80](https://acta.aaenz.no/aaen-studios/calcite#CAL-80)): fields only the server reads, such as a mob variant's `spawn_conditions`, aren't checked, and the world's registries clients aren't sent, worldgen among them, aren't read. Vanilla's own packs pass both, so only a world's pack can tell. Reading every entry costs a boot with no cache about 14 ms more, 92 ms to port-accept where it was 78 on this machine; a boot from the cache, which keeps the contents, is unchanged at 15 ms. | Phase 5 |
| D195 | 2026-11-01 | A chunk's packet is kept while another player is still to be sent it, and let go when the chunks around it or its light change (CAL-70) | A chunk sent with its saved terrain and the engine's light was encoded for each player it was sent to: its sections, heightmaps and light, about 25 KB a chunk, every time. Now the network asks, for each chunk it sends, whether another player in play is still to be sent it (`ChunkSender.waiting`), and if one is, `Level.packet` keeps the bytes and gives the next player a copy. The last player it's encoded for takes it, so a chunk only one player wants is never kept, and packets are kept only while players join or walk over the same chunks. A flat chunk among flat ones is already the flat world's one body, and far chunks are sent as before; neither is kept. A kept packet is let go when a chunk in the 3×3 around it is loaded, changed, accepted or unloaded, since its neighbours' light comes into it, and when the light engine says its light, as a packet sends it, may have changed. `Storage` notes the chunk at `queue`, where light is queued for a section or taken back, and at `swap`, for the sections a run changed, which are the only places what a packet reads can change. The level lets those go before it encodes and at the end of each tick. Blocks set by players don't reach a level's chunks yet; when they do, they let go of the 3×3 the same way. Keeping every packet once encoded would save more, but would hold about 25 KB a chunk for as long as it's loaded, so it isn't done. Compression stays per connection, on the codec threads, and it's most of the work: with 100 bots joining at 20 a second at the spawn of a world vanilla generated, view 10, the server's CPU time is 14 s with vanilla's threshold of 256 and about 2.5 s without, so compressing a shared packet once is its own card ([CAL-81](https://acta.aaenz.no/aaen-studios/calcite#CAL-81)). On that world, the medians of 3 runs, the mean MSPT while joining is 3.24 ms where it was 6.02, and the peak memory 184 MiB where it was 166, from the packets kept while the bots join; the steady MSPT and the chunks and bytes sent are unchanged. On the flat world, where nothing is kept, the steady MSPT is 0.560 ms where it was 0.545, within the runs' noise. Once the Nether and the End load ([CAL-69](https://acta.aaenz.no/aaen-studios/calcite#CAL-69)), only players in the chunk's dimension count. `bench/run.py` now turns the whitelist off: since it defaults to on (D161), the bots had been turned away. | Phase 5 |
| D196 | 2026-11-01 | A chunk's packet kept for several players is compressed once, by the first player's encoder, and the others are sent its body (CAL-81) | Follows D195. Compressing is most of what a chunk costs to send, and each connection compressed its own copy of a kept packet, about 25 KB deflated to about 2.6 KB. Now a packet the level keeps for other players is queued as shared, with its key and a version the level gives each packet it keeps. The encoder that gets it, on a codec thread or on the tick thread for a small flush, compresses it as it would any packet, and hands a copy of the body back with the bytes. The network gives that to the level, which keeps it in the packet's place if the packet is still kept, and lets the packet go. The players after are sent the body, which their encoders only frame and encrypt. Every connection compresses from the server's one threshold, so the body is the same for each and the bytes on the wire are as they were; a body that came to an encoder compressing from another threshold would be left out, with a problem logged. The version keeps a body compressed from a packet that's been let go since, as the light around it changed, from being taken for the new one. Two players sent a chunk in the same tick each compress it, since a body comes back only once the tick's flush is encoded; players joining a tick or more apart, as the bots do, find it. Where the link records or replays, the packet's ID and fields stay with the body, as `sent` checks each packet sent. Compressing a packet once on the tick thread, as it's made, would be simpler, but a chunk takes about 170 µs to compress, which the tick thread can't spare. With 100 bots joining at 20 a second at the spawn of a world vanilla generated, view 10, threshold 256, the medians of 3 runs: the server's CPU time is 3.2 s where it was 16.7, the mean MSPT while joining is 2.10 ms where it was 3.26, as the tick thread copies the body rather than the packet, and the peak memory is 171 MiB where it was 183, as what's kept is a tenth of the size once it's compressed; the steady MSPT, the chunks and the bytes sent are unchanged. On the flat world, where nothing is kept, nothing changes past the runs' noise. | Phase 5 |
| D197 | 2026-11-01 | The level lights a tick's worth of chunks at a time: 50,000 queue entries of light work from one tick's end to the next's, counted, not timed (CAL-82) | With 100 bots joining a world vanilla generated, the worst tick while they joined was about 40 ms in every run, close to the 50 ms budget. It was the first bot's view: about 390 chunks back from the IO thread at once, nearly all of the tick in lighting the 336 whose light wasn't saved, at 90 to 260 µs each. Vanilla lights on a thread of its own, a batch of tasks at a time, so its tick never waits for it; Calcite lights on the tick thread (D184), and so did all of them in the pass they came ready. Now the chunks ready to be lit are lit in the order they came to be, each with a run of its own, and the queue entries the runs go through count against `LIGHT_PER_TICK`; once it's spent, the rest wait for the next tick. A chunk saved lit costs nothing, as its light is only read. The budget covers the reads handed back between ticks, the tick and the players' part of it, so `Network.tick` ends with `Level.tick_end()`, which lets the next tick's worth be done. Counting chunks was tried first, at 64 and then 32 a tick: a chunk's work varies about 140 times over, from 256 entries, one a column, to 36,432 on the vanilla world (a median of 256, p99 16,874), so 32 chunks still made 9 ms ticks, and a fresh flat world's spawn of 49 chunks would have waited a tick more than vanilla's. A time budget would light differently from run to run, which a replay can't take. At about 70 ns an entry, 50,000 are about 3.5 ms, and a flat world's spawn, 49 chunks at 256, is lit in one tick as before; a spawn on terrain not saved lit can take one more. Lighting one chunk at a time gives the same light as lighting them together, since a run settles on the one answer for the blocks it has; the light tests and the replays agree. The flat world's worst joining tick had gone from 2.0 ms at 9b489c6 to 4.4 at d30ab20, when chunks began to be read on threads and came back in a burst, then to 10.6 at 3b8d324, when the level began to light them. With 100 bots joining at 20 a second, view 10, the medians of 3 runs: on the vanilla world, the worst tick while joining is 7.9 ms where it was 41.0, and the mean 2.16 ms where it was 2.13; on the flat world, 4.8 ms where it was 10.6, and the mean 1.29 where it was 1.28. Every bot has all its chunks as soon as before, and the steady MSPT, chunks and bytes are unchanged. The CPU time varied from 1.1 to 3.7 s between runs of one build, and showed no change past that. What's left in the worst tick is the light, the 200 chunks that came told to the light engine, about 1.7 ms, and the distance manager's updates, about 1.9 ms. | Phase 5 |
| D198 | 2026-11-01 | The `noise` probe writes every NaN as `0x7fc00000`, `Float.floatToIntBits`'s NaN, so a NaN's sign and payload aren't compared (CAL-68) | Calcite's noises matched vanilla's on 978 of the 996 cases at first, and the 18 others differed only in a NaN's sign bit, at inputs such as (-inf, inf, NaN). Java doesn't say which NaN an operation gives when two NaNs meet, and x86 gives the first operand's, so it depends on the order the JIT or the interpreter puts them in, not on the game; LLVM is free to swap the operands of an addition too. Worldgen never samples a noise at an infinite or NaN coordinate, so matching the sign would mean copying one JIT's register choices for inputs the game can't make. Where a NaN comes out is still compared, and every finite and infinite value bit for bit. Other probes that test arithmetic on NaN itself (`math`, `mth`) keep the raw bits | Phase 5 |
| D199 | 2026-11-01 | Density functions are hash-consed: two functions vanilla's records call equal are one node, and a node's range, axes, rewrites and sampler are each worked out once (CAL-68) | Vanilla keys its compiler by function: `getSampler` keeps a sampler per function, and `preparedCaches` a cache per cache's input, both by `equals` of the records, so two caches written out alike in two files are one cache. Calcite makes each node once, keyed by what the records compare: floats by `Float.compare`, so `-0.0` isn't `0.0` and every NaN is one, an interval select's thresholds by `==`, where they are one, a spline's points by `Arrays.equals`, references and the registry's noises by ID, and a noise written out in place by itself, since its holder has no ID. Then a map keyed by function is a map keyed by index, and the optimizer's passes, which build the same trees again and again, cost a lookup where a tree is met twice. Remembering a range or a sampler changes nothing vanilla gives: each is worked out the same way every time, and one that throws throws the first time it's asked for, in vanilla's order, so the case's exception is the same. All 828 `density` cases match on Windows and Linux. |

| D283 | 2026-11-03 | Regions (CAL-63, P5.4): the layout, the tick's phases and the determinism check now; a region's own state and its parallel loop come with what Phase 6 gives regions to tick. **The layout.** `server.layout` puts each player in a region by D47's rule over the squares of their views, with a merge distance of 9 chunks, D53's least, in each dimension, so players whose views come within 9 chunks of each other, or of someone between them, are one region. It's worked out at the barrier before the world phase, from where everyone is and the last layout alone, so the same input log gives the same regions on any number of threads (D22): a region keeps its ID as it changes, a split keeps it for the part with the longest-joined player and gives the others new ones, and a merge keeps the smallest. **The phases.** The world phase sends each region's players their chunks in turn, by ID, and each region's players by entity ID. Packets are handled as they come between ticks, which is D50's packet phase run in the wait: vanilla runs its packets' tasks as they come while it waits for the next tick (`waitUntilNextTick`), so each is handled before the tick it came before either way, and a client hears back at once, as from vanilla, not up to a tick later. **The check.** `rig/lockstep.py`, in CI on both systems, records a session with 8 chunk workers and 4 bots walking into new terrain from the start, then replays it, which runs the generator and light on its own thread, and fails if a packet sent or a line logged differs (G5.3) | Measured first: 16 bots at view 32 joining a pregenerated world all at once had every chunk, 69,277 of them, in 4.1 s, and the server thread worked 630 ms of that: 343 ms sending, mostly copying a kept chunk's packet for each player, and 237 ms taking back what the codec, IO and light threads finished. The joins were held by the client's own pace, 64 chunks a tick, not the server. So there's nothing yet a region would run that's worth a thread, and a parallel loop over regions would cost its threads' wake-ups each tick for no gain. Entities, block ticks and block entities are what regions tick, and they come in Phase 6, with the chunks they're in owned by their regions: that's when the world phase becomes a `parallel for` over regions, with std.mail between them ([CAL-101](https://acta.aaenz.no/aaen-studios/calcite#CAL-101)). The check passed with 8 workers and 4 bots, 400 ticks and 7,900 packets and lines compared, and with 16 workers and 8 bots, 600 ticks and 21,581, with no difference |
